#326. Google pushes privacy angle as Gemini-powered smartglasses debut starting at $395

The first Gemini smartglasses are a collab between Google, Samsung, Gentle Monster, and Warby Parker.

These devices are a collaboration between Google, Samsung, and the aforementioned eyewear designers. Gentle Monster and Warby Parker have each designed a few different frames, with options for transparent and tinted lenses. Samsung is providing the computing hardware that fits inside them, and Google has the software covered.

Warby Parker has designed three frames for Google eyewear, called Alden, Reid, and Wren (see above). They are available with both standard optical and sun lenses in Pine, Saddle, and Jet Black colors at $395 and higher. Warby Parker is only selling devices in the US. Gentle Monster has gone a bit further with six styles, but Google hasn’t told us any names for those yet. They come in dark red, pink, gray, and yellow colors and are available with clear, tinted, blue, or brown lenses. The Gentle Monster styles start a little higher at $449, and they will be available in the US and South Korea.

Google says the frames all weigh about 41 grams, which is not terrible but is definitely near the top for glasses generally. They will, naturally, weigh much more when you account for lenses. That will also determine the final pricing. So far, we only have the starting prices for these devices, including non-prescription lenses.

The setup here is similar to the Ray-Ban Meta glasses we’ve seen for a few years. There are no screens in the lenses, but you can interact with the AI system via voice or by activating the integrated camera. The experience should be similar to using Gemini Live on your phone, but it will be a lot easier to fire up the AI. You can say “Hey Gemini” or tap a touch-sensitive area of the frame to start interacting with the robot.

Credit: Gentle Monster Credit: Gentle Monster

Since this is the same chatbot that operates across the Google ecosystem, it has access to your data and preferences. You can ask questions and get personalized answers, have Google Maps guide you by voice, or just talk with the AI to kill time. The glasses use custom open-ear speakers to limit distractions for those around you. Google notes the directional microphones should not pick up speech from other people, either.

Adding the camera feed is where things get interesting. When the AI can see what you see, it has the potential to do more work on your behalf. Google suggests asking Gemini for recommendations when looking at a bookshelf or glancing at an event poster to have the AI add it to your calendar. The glasses will also support Gemini Guided Vision, which provides conversational audio descriptions for people who are visually impaired.

The utility of these devices hinges on Gemini. I would be remiss not to point out that Gemini, like all AI systems, is not infallible. In fact, it can be frustratingly fallible at times. The chatbot still regularly screws up simple things like adding calendar appointments or identifying objects in an image in my testing, and when you don’t have a screen to verify the AI’s output, it may be harder to trust it. These devices will really only make sense if you can rely on Gemini to do things right, and that’s still not a guarantee.

For starters, there’s a recording light that is on whenever the camera is active. Like the Meta glasses, Google’s eyewear will detect if the light has been covered up or modified in any way that would prevent it from illuminating. In that event, the glasses will refuse to activate the camera. All Google-powered glasses will have a physical off switch, too.

Credit: Warby Parker Credit: Warby Parker

The terms of use for Gemini eyewear also require users to agree not to record people without consent or in sensitive areas. If Google finds out someone has broken those rules, it may remotely disable the glasses. Google is going a bit further in restricting video functionality than Meta, too. Google says it will not allow any apps in the Play Store to live stream video to social media from the glasses (not even YouTube live is supported). The content you record is saved to your gallery, and you can upload that wherever you want, but going live is apparently not on the agenda. Real-time facial recognition is also off the table, according to Google.  The glasses also automatically redact faces before sending video to Google for processing.

Data on the glasses is encrypted, and it remains that way during transit to the cloud. However, it’s not encrypted on Google’s servers. The company does note that it’s working to bring Private AI Compute to glasses, which will isolate data during processing so not even Google can access it.

Credit: Gentle Monster Credit: Gentle Monster

#323. Zelenskyy says Starshield has been slowed again, endangering Ukraine's defense

It comes three weeks after Ukraine’s speeds were restored following the last throttling incident.

As media headlines focus on Jensen Huang's resignation and the midterms fallout, you could almost forget that Ukraine is still fighting for its life — with all of Europe nervously watching. According to President Volodymyr Zelenskyy, his job just got harder again. The country's access to Starshield, the military version of Starlink, has been hit with "systematic speed restrictions" for several days. And it just so happens to be while Trump's envoys are in Moscow for talks with Russian President Vladimir Putin.

Zelenskyy posted on X that the speed limits are being imposed along the war's entire front line and in Russian-occupied territory. He said any terminal moving faster than 161 km/h (100 mph) loses its connection to the satellite network. Following briefings from his defense minister and top commander, Zelenskyy said Ukraine's military had investigated and ruled out random errors.

"Unfortunately, this is helping the Russians kill more Ukrainians and carry out more accurate strikes against Ukraine," Zelenskyy said. "We are trying to establish all the circumstances with the American side."

Ukraine's interceptor drones rely on Starshield to chase down Russia's jet-powered Shahed drones, which can fly up to 500 km/h (310 mph). Trying to catch them at 100 mph won't cut it. Zelenskyy says some strikes have already gotten through because Ukraine's interceptors couldn't go fast enough.

The timing is... curious. US envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner landed in Moscow today for talks with Putin. They're expected to meet Zelenskyy later at a summit in Ireland.

This isn't the first time Ukraine's Starshield access has been throttled. On October 14, Ukraine's military found that its speeds were being limited to around the same threshold. It happened as President Trump was publicly complaining about Ukraine's strikes on Russian refineries, which he blamed for high fuel prices in the US ahead of the midterms. Analysts agree that Trump's war with Iran has been the biggest factor in sky-high oil and diesel prices.

It still isn't clear who made the call to throttle Starshield the first time. Reuters, citing US officials, reported that the order came from inside the White House. But days later, the Wall Street Journal reported that Musk personally ordered the slowdown, possibly after talking with Trump. Trump denied any role, for whatever that's worth.

Musk's responses were all over the place. First, he said Starshield is "owned and controlled by the US government," then later claimed Ukraine had violated Starlink's terms of service. That's a strange thing to say about something you just said you don't own or control. Regardless, speeds were restored on October 24, reportedly after Trump called Musk and asked him to lift the caps amid bipartisan pressure from lawmakers.

Three weeks later, we're back where we started, with (so far) no one claiming responsibility.

The allegations emerged after a judge decided on Nov. 3 that The Guardian newspaper and ITV News, a British broadcaster, could publish the claims against Mr. Walliams.

Mr. Walliams denies the allegations. In a letter sent to news outlets, he said he accepted he had “behaved thoughtlessly towards other people I had casual sexual relationships with,” but he added: “I am not a sexual predator. I have not broken the law.”

The secrecy had driven a swirl of online speculation in recent weeks about the identity of the unnamed celebrity.

In recent years, he has been dogged by accusations over his professional conduct, including reports that he had made derogatory and sexually explicit remarks about contestants during the recording of an episode of the television show “Britain’s Got Talent,” for which he was a judge.

The investigation that The Guardian and ITV News published on Tuesday, which the two organizations had worked on since April 2023, was based on accounts from 30 people who accused Mr. Walliams of abusing his power to sexually prey on women and targeting teenagers, some of whom met him when they were as young as 15.

#320. The amazing disappearing exoplanet

An exoplanet seems to have stopped transiting in front of its host star.

One nice thing about discovering exoplanets is that, once you work out how long their orbits take, they continue to show up like clockwork. The timing is so regular, in fact, that we can use variations to detect the gravitational influence of additional planets in the same exosolar system. And the precise timing is useful in that we can rely on it to point telescopes at the system when we know some of the light we detect has passed through the planet’s atmosphere.

On Monday, researchers reported on an exoplanet that simply stopped showing up. TOI-2535b is a hot Jupiter-sized planet we’ve been observing as it transits between its host star, TOI-2535, and Earth for about seven years now. But it recently stopped showing up, and researchers suspect that’s due to an unseen companion orbiting in a different plane.

TOI-2535 is one of the many stars with exoplanet candidates identified by TESS, the Transiting Exoplanet Survey Satellite. It’s a K dwarf, meaning it’s somewhat smaller than the Sun. And TESS found signs of a Jupiter-sized planet orbiting it every eight days, an orbit close enough to make TOI-2535b a “hot Jupiter” exoplanet. At that point, there was nothing especially unusual about it.

But a closer look at the data showed two very strange things. First, the timing of when its transits occurred could shift by as much as six hours, a substantial variation for an orbit this brief. That’s typically a sign of gravitational interactions with another planet; depending on the details of that second planet’s orbit, it may slow the first one or accelerate it through a transit more quickly. Since the second planet doesn’t show up in TESS data, it appears to orbit in a slightly different plane, so it doesn’t pass between its host star and Earth.

The second oddity is that the transit duration grew progressively shorter in more recent observations. This suggests that, while early observations saw the planet transiting across the star’s equator, later ones involved transits near one of the poles, ensuring that the planet spent less time between the star and Earth. This trend eventually reached its logical end point: TOI-2535b simply stopped transiting in the most recent observations.

Obviously, gravitational interactions with a planet orbiting in a different plane could potentially alter TOI-2535b’s orbit so it no longer passes between TOI-2535 and Earth. There is also some precedent for the converse happening: a star around which no transits were originally detected but where transits began appearing in later observations. Still, as a Jupiter-sized planet, TOI-2535b should not be that easy to shift, and as the researchers note, “This is the fastest rate of inclination change observed for an exoplanet orbiting a single star.”

The second, unseen planet “would need to be either extremely massive or on a highly eccentric orbit,” the researchers noted. So they decided to figure out what sorts of configurations could lead to such rapid changes.

To do so, they modeled 300,000 different starting configurations and ran them forward in time. They found there wasn’t a single favored configuration; as long as the planets were in an orbital resonance, things were relatively stable, at least over relatively short periods. So they took the 100 best solutions and ran them forward for a million years.

About two-thirds became unstable during that period, which ruled out a couple of the potential resonances. The remaining third shared a couple of features. One is that the second, undetected planet has to be pretty massive—roughly Saturn-sized at a minimum. The second is that the angle between the orbits of the two planets can’t be that large. If it is, the difference would shrink over time, causing their orbits to become increasingly eccentric. Eventually, this results in a close approach between the two planets, followed by instability.

The University of California, Los Angeles has posted a video of one of the better models if you’re having a hard time visualizing all of this.

While a second planet hasn’t been detected via the transit method, its gravitational pull on the star should create changes in the radial velocity we could potentially detect. Right now, however, we don’t have enough clean data to detect it. The researchers also say there’s enough noise in the transit timing variations that a third planet in the system can’t be excluded.

The question we probably can’t answer with further observations is how this system got into its current state. Large planets are thought to form farther out from the star and then migrate inward because of friction caused by the gas in the planet-forming disk. (This inward motion tends to stop when orbital resonances develop.) But this friction should also limit the angle between the planets’ orbits. Without some additional event that mixed up the orbits, it’s not obvious how this system could have come to be.

The Astrophysical Journal Letters, 2026. DOI: 10.3847/2041-8213/aeb4a4  (About DOIs).

#319. Ex-NVIDIA CEO Jensen Huang is set to testify before Congress this month

House Foreign Affairs Chair Brian Mast said the hearing will take place before Thanksgiving.

Jensen Huang might have stepped down from his roles at the top of NVIDIA, but he won't be sliding out of the spotlight just yet. The company's former CEO and president is set to testify before Congress by Thanksgiving, according to House representatives.

House Foreign Affairs Chairman Brian Mast said Huang agreed to testify on Wednesday — hours before NVIDIA announced his resignation with immediate effect — after lawmakers threatened him with a subpoena. It appears the hearing will take place behind closed doors (which is unfortunate, because that's likely to be some spicy testimony). However, Mast expects it to be filmed and transcribed, according to Bloomberg. The House's Intelligence and China-focused committees will join the Foreign Affairs cohort for the hearing.

"Jensen and I have been very at odds as some of you already know on export controls, but NVIDIA is a very important company to the United States of America," Mast said. "I don't want to see anything hurt in NVIDIA, but he has some very serious questions to answer."

NVIDIA has been in turmoil after it emerged over the weekend that the company was investigating Huang over an affair to which he admitted, allegedly with a woman suspected of being a Chinese spy. President Donald Trump said the FBI is investigating the situation as well.

On Wednesday, it was reported that Huang had another affair, this time with Kimberly Powell, an NVIDIA executive who reported to him. The company's board met later that day to determine Huang's future. Colette Kress, NVIDIA's executive vice president and chief financial officer, is stepping in as interim CEO while the company seeks a permanent successor.

Meanwhile, the Department of Justice is reportedly investigating Alice Huang, the alleged spy, to determine whether entities associated with her were involved in attempts to smuggle Super Micro Computer products to China. The DoJ is also said to be taking the lead in an investigation into her former company, the Singapore-based Megaspeed International, over alleged smuggling of US-made chips into China in defiance of export restrictions. The company said it would cooperate with the probe.

Officials in Singapore opened an investigation into Alice Huang, Megaspeed and associated individuals last year. The Ministry of Home Affairs said in a statement on Thursday that she was barred from re-entering the country after police determined she knowingly offered up inaccurate information to downplay her role in Megaspeed. However, they "concluded that there was insufficient evidence to establish that Huang had committed any offences relating to the possible inaccurate declarations for which Megaspeed was being investigated." The investigation into Megaspeed and six other individuals is ongoing.

#318. Chelsea searches for new manager as Premier League breaks record for most sackings in a single season

It was just six months ago that Graham Potter was deemed the man to usher in a fresh, exciting era at Chelsea.

The club’s new ownership group, led by American businessman Todd Boehly, had enticed Potter away from Brighton with the promise of managing one of the most lucrative teams in world soccer.

More than $600 million was spent on bringing big-name players to the club and Potter, one of the most exciting young managers in the Premier League, was to mold them into a winning machine.

Chelsea has spent over $600 million on new signings. Here's why that's significant

But, on Sunday, the club announced that it had sacked its manager after an underwhelming spell in charge.

Potter managed to win just 10 of the 28 league matches this season and leaves the team 11th in the Premier League table.

In recent weeks, players had looked a shadow of their former selves and many fans had lost confidence that Potter’s plan for the club could ever work.

Eventually, after previously sticking by its manager, Blues ownership agreed.

“On behalf of everyone at the club, we want to thank Graham sincerely for his contribution to Chelsea,” the club’s co-owners Todd Boehly and Behdad Eghbali said in a statement.

“We have the highest degree of respect for Graham as a coach and as a person. He has always conducted himself with professionalism and integrity and we are all disappointed in this outcome.”

In his previous role as Brighton manager, Potter was given time to stamp his identity on the squad.

The club was punching above its weight financially and, as a result, carefully selected new signings which would compliment Potter’s style of play.

The approach paid off as the team became an established Premier League outfit which has gone on to greater things this season – the Seagulls currently sit sixth in the league and in the UEFA Conference League qualification spot.

At Chelsea, it seemed the complete opposite.

As was the case under previous owner Roman Abramovich, the club expected instant results and used its financial weight to sign a number of high-profile names, including the likes of Enzo Fernández and Mykhailo Mudryk.

Despite his impressive CV, Potter had never managed superstars and suddenly found himself in a changing room with multiple.

The scatter gun approach to recruitment also left the team lacking balance and organization with Potter failing to get a tune out of a team busting with talent.

In truth, many had raised an eyebrow when Potter, inexperienced at the very top level, was chosen to replace Thomas Tuchel as manager.

Despite falling out of favor with fans by the end of his tenure, Tuchel had guided Chelsea to the Champions League title in 2021 and is still considered by many as one of the best coaches in the world.

The German has gone on to sign with Bavarian giant Bayern Munich through the 2024/25 season and recently told reporters he needed “quite a bit of time to distance himself from Chelsea” because leaving the club had hurt him.

Bayern Munich sacks manager Julian Nagelsmann, appoints Thomas Tuchel

“Todd [Boehly] said he would be different to Roman [Abramovich],” former Liverpool player and current pundit Jamie Carragher wrote on Twitter.

“I feel for Graham Potter, but it was inevitable. You don’t change Tuchel for Potter. Ridiculous decision to start with.”

With 10 games left in the league and a Champions League quarterfinal against Real Madrid on the horizon, Chelsea still has plenty to play for.

Assistant coach Bruno Saltor will take over on an interim basis, but Boehly and his team must now look for a long-term replacement capable – and willing – to bring order to Chelsea’s chaos.

Potter’s sacking is yet another sign of just how transient managers can be in the top-flight of English football, especially when results aren’t instantaneous.

There has now been a record 12 sackings in the Premier League so far this season, with Potter leaving his position the same day Leicester boss Brendan Rodgers was sacked from his.

The previous record was 10 manager changes in a single season, set in four other campaigns, most recently in 2017/18.

#317. Multiple Microsoft 365 services are experiencing an outage

Outlook and Teams are among the platforms impacted by “elevated load” issues.

Microsoft is experiencing an outage for several of its services this afternoon. Initial reports focused on users being unable to access their email in Outlook, but more than the one platform in its Microsoft 365 suite appears to be down. The company's support account on X acknowledged the issue.

At 2:15PM ET, many Engadget staff were unable to even load Microsoft's service status page. Those who were saw a message that listed Exchange Online, Microsoft Teams, SharePoint Online, Microsoft Copilot 365 and Microsoft Defender XDR as the potentially impacted services. While the status report points to Business and Enterprise tiers of the 365 tools, individual users also appear to be impacted. The full update states:

"We're investigating an issue impacting multiple Microsoft 365 services and we've determined the affected infrastructure is experiencing elevated load across multiple regions, which is likely contributing to the connectivity errors. We're restarting and repairing impacted portions of this infrastructure to restore capacity, and reviewing an unexpected increase in a specific type of service traffic to determine whether it's contributing to the elevated load. In parallel, we're evaluating options to redirect traffic to alternate infrastructure to expedite relief."

Around 5PM ET, Microsoft Planner, Microsoft To Do, Microsoft Intune, Microsoft Defender for Office 365 and Microsoft Purview had been added to the list of impacted services. Issues with Power BI and Microsoft 365 Apps Admin Center were also noted. The latest update reveals that a fix for this afternoon's disruptions still may not be imminent.

"We're continuing to pursue all available pathways to restore service, as our actions taken thus far have not provided the anticipated relief," the status page states. "Our investigation has found that a core process on the affected servers is becoming overwhelmed, which causes the servers to stop responding and contributes to the capacity loss."

#315. Who Is David Walliams, the UK Entertainer Accused of Sexual Misconduct?

The British entertainer David Walliams, who was publicly accused on Tuesday of a pattern of sexual misconduct, is a comedian and prolific author of children’s books who has been a fixture on Britain’s television screens for more than 20 years.

Mr. Walliams, 55, rose to fame as a sketch show comedian in the 2000s and later as a judge on “Britain’s Got Talent.” He is also one of the country’s most successful children’s book authors, publishing over 40 titles that have sold tens of millions of copies.

The show pushed the boundaries of acceptability, even during a period of provocative comedy television in Britain. Mr. Walliams and Mr. Lucas donned fat suits, caked themselves in makeup and wore blackface.

#314. Man's secret use of synthetic testosterone for cows nearly destroyed his heart

Doctors warn that such life-threatening cases are becoming more common.

For 11 years, a Massachusetts man had an alarming array of health problems, most notably that his heart kept failing. He landed in the hospital over and over again, needed life support at one point, spent years going to specialists, and underwent a barrage of tests along the way.

But through it all, he never revealed a key detail behind his ailments: He was taking powerful, illicit synthetic testosterone derivatives (aka anabolic-androgenic steroids, or AAS). One of the drugs he took was approved only for use in animals; it’s primarily used to beef up cows.

Left in the dark, his doctors had to puzzle this out on their own. In a case report in this week’s issue of the New England Journal of Medicine, they explain how they did it. They also offer a warning: These cases will only become more common, and clinicians need to know what they look like.

“Use of AAS has now become a major international substance use disorder,” they wrote. In the US, it’s estimated that between 2.9 and 4 million people, mostly male athletes, use AAS. To put this in a public health perspective, this is more than the number of people in the US with an HIV infection.

“Clinicians should be prepared to encounter increasing numbers of patients presenting with the long-term effects of AAS exposure, including cardiomyopathy, atherosclerotic disease, and anabolic steroid–induced hypogonadism.”

Patients also need to be prepared for what recovery looks like, which they described as being “lengthy and varied.” The man’s case offers a stark example.

The problems started when he was 39. He fell ill after returning from a trip to the Caribbean and went to Massachusetts General Hospital in Boston with a fever, chills, and trouble breathing. Tests suggested he had an acute kidney injury, liver damage, and an injured heart. Testing of his heart showed that the left chamber was pumping out just 21 percent of its blood in a beat (a normal amount is above 50 percent). His heart only got worse from there, and within hours, he ended up on a type of life support called ECMO (venoarterial extracorporeal membrane oxygenation), a machine that does the work of both the heart and lungs in dire circumstances. He luckily recovered—and quickly. But doctors were baffled by the cause.

When he was weaned off ECMO a few days later, his heart function normalized. Doctors looked for evidence of anything that could have sparked the sudden cardiac spiral. They found nothing but a mildly elevated level of antibodies against a toxin made by Streptococcus bacteria. In rare instances, an infection with these germs can lead to a condition called poststreptococcal myocarditis, which could potentially explain some of his symptoms. It was their best guess. They prescribed him antibiotics, as well as drugs to lower his blood pressure.

Two years later, he went to another hospital. This time it was for acute pain in his abdomen and legs. Doctors found signs of cell death in his kidneys. The arteries that supply blood to his kidneys appeared narrowed, suggesting a condition called renal-artery stenosis. There were also signs of inflammation in his blood vessels. But the doctors at the hospital couldn’t pinpoint the problem. They prescribed him anti-inflammatory drugs and sent him on his way.

For the next five years, the man started seeing a cadre of specialists to monitor his health, including doctors in primary care, cardiology, nephrology, rheumatology, general medicine, sleep medicine, and psychiatry. When he was about 46, he reported fatigue to his primary care doctor, who found that his testosterone level was low. The man then went to an endocrinologist, reporting not just fatigue but low libido and poor sleep. The endocrinologist recommended treatment for obstructive sleep apnea, which can increase the risk of low testosterone.

A year later, the man added a hematologist to his rotation of care providers. A routine blood test revealed abnormally high levels of red blood cells (erythrocytosis). The hematologist noted the man also had high blood pressure (150/108 mm Hg) but found everything else to be normal. The hematologist ended up attributing the erythrocytosis to the man’s sleep apnea and renal-artery stenosis.

It wasn’t until he arrived again at Mass General in 2026 that doctors finally put it all together. Now 50, the man was once again experiencing heart failure—and he had again just returned from the Caribbean.

In the case report, doctors provided a more complete picture of the man, noting that he lived with his wife and children in eastern Massachusetts, worked as a facilities manager, and described himself as a workout “fanatic.” He used tanning beds and applied spray tans. He told doctors he had erectile dysfunction and reduced libido for several years. They also noticed that he had mildly enlarged breast tissue (a condition called gynecomastia), progressive insulin resistance, decreased levels of “good” cholesterol (HDL or high-density lipoprotein), and fatty liver disease.

When he arrived at the hospital, he reported five days of worsening chest tightness and pressure, fatigue, swelling in his legs, and trouble breathing. His heart was racing at 134 beats per minute. His blood pressure was high at 146/106 mm Hg. Doctors described him as “muscular, tanned, anxious, and in mild distress.” Tests showed his heart was once again failing, with the left chamber pumping out just 20 percent of its blood.

The doctors started reviewing a long list of possible explanations. They ruled out infectious diseases that can cause heart inflammation (myocarditis), as well as autoimmune conditions, medication side effects, and toxins. The explanation they were left with—which also seemed to explain much of his other problems—was high doses of testosterone, or perhaps an AAS.

Illicit use of AAS is clearly linked to the heart problems, including the decreased blood pumping the man had, as well as strain and damage. It could also explain his enlarged breast tissue, fatty liver and signs of liver damage, progressive insulin resistance, kidney injuries, high blood pressure, and high levels of red blood cells (erythrocytosis). The sleep problems, fatigue, and low testosterone levels—which doctors previously guessed were from obstructive sleep apnea—could actually be from a drop in natural hormone production after doses of AAS (a condition called hypogonadism).

In the man’s lengthy medical history, he had never mentioned using AAS or testosterone supplements. Doctors prepared to have a delicate conversation to determine if he was secretly using the drugs. When they finally broached the topic, he confessed; he had been intermittently using high doses of AAS to boost his muscles, taking the most recent dose before his vacation to the Caribbean. The testosterone derivative he used was trenbolone, an AAS only approved for use in veterinary medicine; it’s mostly used in livestock.

The doctors then tested his total testosterone level, finding it was so high that the test couldn’t accurately measure it. The assay’s range of detection was 12 to 1,500 nanograms per deciliter of blood. His level was over 1,500 ng/dl. The range considered normal for the test was between 249 and 836 ng/dl. The doctors gave their final diagnosis: androgen excess–related cardiomyopathy.

The primary treatment for his heart is to stop taking AAS. Doctors prescribed him medications for high blood pressure, heart failure, and diabetes. Four months later, heart tests showed his left chamber was pumping 49 percent of its blood, a significant improvement. The state of his liver also improved. But recovery from the AAS-induced hypogonadism was a much longer, more difficult journey. It was made harder because he was over age 35, had taken AAS for more than a decade, and had preexisting dysfunction.

Six months into his recovery, he still had significant hypogonadism symptoms, which include loss of sexual desire, erectile dysfunction, gynecomastia, reduced orgasmic satisfaction, and ejaculatory disorders. He opted to try a drug called clomiphene, which binds to estrogen receptors. It’s often used in women to help with fertility, but in men it is sometimes used to try to jumpstart natural testosterone production. It didn’t work for the man. He moved on to trying topical testosterone-replacement therapy but continued to have erectile dysfunction. Doctors warn that it could take up to three years for the hypogonadism to resolve.

In the last follow-up at eight months, he reported that he was still off AAS and had no symptoms of heart failure.

#313. Who is Collette Kress, NVIDIA's interim CEO?

Kress spent 25 years in various finance roles before joining NVIDIA in 2013

After 33 years as the chief executive officer of NVIDIA, Jensen Huang has resigned from the AI and gaming giant he founded. On Thursday morning, the company's board of director announced Colette Kress, NVIDIA's current executive vice president and chief financial officer, as Huang's temporary replacement.

"The Board has full confidence in Colette and the leadership team, and we expect the company's operations, customer commitments and product roadmap to continue without interruption," newly-minted NVIDIA board chair Harvey Jones said in a release announcing Thursday's news.

Kress is a relative unknown, but is taking lead of NVIDIA at tumultuous time in the company's history. Huang departure comes days after NVIDIA's board said it would investigate Huang after he admitted to having an affair with a former tech executive accused of being a Chinese intelligence asset. The New York Times later reported that Huang had also had an affair with an NVIDIA executive who reported to him. Before the revelations, the company did not have a succession plan in place. And for at least the immediate future, it's now on Kress to lead the company. Here's what we know about the executive.

Kress worked at three companies before joining NVIDIA. She first spent eight years in finance roles at Texas Instruments before joining Microsoft in 1997. In 2006, six years after Steve Ballmer took over as Microsoft's CEO, she became the chief financial officer of the company's Server and Tools division. She remained with the company until 2010, when she moved to Cisco. There, she was the senior vice president and chief financial officer of the company's Business Technology and Operations Finance unit.

To hear Kress tell it, Huang went out of his way to recruit her. Sometime before she joined NVIDIA in 2013, the CEO apparently sketched his vision for the company on a whiteboard. Per a 2023 interview, Huang wanted to transform NVIDIA into the dominant player in the nascent AI industry. Say what you will about Huang's personal life and judgement, the man was an AI visionary.

It would be almost another four years following Kress joining NVIDIA before a group of Google researchers published "Attention Is All You Need," the paper that introduced the world to transformer models, and nearly a decade before the release of ChatGPT. Kress partly credits her sons, who were only eight and 10 at the time, for buying Huang's pitch. Despite their young age, the two already knew of NVIDIA and its importance to gaming. In a 2024 Fortune interview, Kress said NVIDIA was the smallest company she had worked for when she joined in 2013.

At the start of her tenure, NVIDIA's revenue was $4.28 billion for the year. In fiscal 2026, the company recorded a record $215.9 billion in revenue.

When NVIDIA's board of directors announced Kress as Huang's temporary successor, it said it would consider both internal and external candidates to take on the role of CEO permanently, leaving the door open for her and other current NVIDIA employees to win the job. In any case, it's far too early to speculate who the company's board might interview for the job. But if you can forgive a writer a tired analogy, if Kress stays on as CEO, she is likely to be a Tim Cook-like figure, one that can stabilize the company and guide it toward continued growth.

"The Board, [executive staff] and I recognize that this has been an extraordinarily difficult period and I want you to know how grateful we are for your resilience, professionalism and continued dedication," Kress wrote in her first email to NVIDIA employees after the transition was announced, according to CNBC.

Still, she has a very difficult job ahead of her. Huang has agreed to testify before Congress in a hearing that the House aims to hold before the Thanksgiving holiday. The chair of the House Foreign Affairs committee, Brian Mast of Florida, said he wasn't ruling out the possibility of "public hearings for other people or other things down road." You can bet NVIDIA is likely to face an intense amount of scrutiny in the coming weeks, particularly when Democrats take control of the House and Senate at the start of next year.

#311. Meta's Muse can now make calls on your behalf (if you have a paid subscription)

The AI agent can schedule appointments, make reservations and wait on hold for you.

Meta's Muse just got another ability that could make the AI agent feel a little bit more like an actual personal assistant. Muse is now able to place phone calls on your behalf, if you have a paid subscription.

With the change, Muse is able to call businesses to help with tasks like making reservations, scheduling appointments and other "calls you don't want to make," Meta's Chief AI officer Alexandr Wang said in an update. The feature is available to those who have paid for a separate Muse subscription, which comes with additional tokens and some extra features.

Some users who have had access to the feature already have reported that Muse has been able to handle phone-related tasks with relative ease. Moltbook creator and Meta Superintelligence employee Matt Schlicht shared a recreation of a phone conversation his agent had with a Volvo dealership last month, noting that it was able to wait on hold in order to get the info he needed.

My muse called Volvo for me to see when the next available appointment is.

I have it do multiple calls like this every day (I'm still trying to get better and better at leveraging muse).

My muse called, waited on the line for 15 minutes,... pic.twitter.com/bosH1tyW1R

— Matt Schlicht (@MattPRD) October 9, 2026

Interestingly, in the call shared by Schlicht, Muse doesn't explicitly identify itself as an AI agent though it notes it's calling his behalf and that it's transcribing the call. That may be enough for some straightforward tasks, but there's always a chance these types of calls could go off the rails or the person on the other end hangs up when they realize they're speaking with an AI.

Meta has been steadily churning out new features for Muse, which has already drawn more than 10 million users, according to the company. People can also now assign a dedicated email address to their agent in order to loop it into important emails without giving wholesale access to their inbox.

The company isn't the first to experiment with phone-calling abilities for AI. Google has introduced a number of similar features over the years, including "Call For Me" for Pixel phones and Instinct recently introduced a "Concierge" feature for its agent that's meant to handle more complex calls.

#308. Building a rocket is hard. AST SpaceMobile thinks it's "simple."

“We’re not doing this thing because we like rockets.”

During an hour-long event on Thursday, Abel Avellan, the founder and chief executive of the telecommunications company AST SpaceMobile, laid out his vision for developing a large rocket to launch his Bluebird satellites into orbit.

Avellan emphasized, over and over again, that AST plans to build a “simple” rocket. He paraphrased SpaceX founder Elon Musk several times, saying, “the best part is no part.” Avellan emphasized that AST has already procured engines for its proposed Blacklift rocket, asserting that engine development accounts for 80 percent of a typical rocket’s development cycle.

“Of course rocket engineers are going to get mad with me, but you can simplify it as a big tank feeding engines,” he said of the rocket. “So big tanks—we know how to do them. We know how to do them. We know how to do them in composite.”

I am not a rocket engineer, and I am not mad at Avellan. But he sure seems to be substantially underestimating the challenge of building rockets.

Avellan also has a problem. AST SpaceMobile appears to be successful in building very large satellites, Bluebirds, that are capable of providing direct-to-cell capability from an orbit about 550km above Earth. The problem is there is a commercial launch crunch, and the company is struggling to get its satellites on orbit today, and projects to do so into the future.

“We’re not doing this thing because we like rockets,” he said during the presentation. “We’re not doing this thing because we want to be in the launch business. We want this because we have so much demand of our satellites, and as you have seen it, we cannot get enough launches.”

The Blacklift rocket will be “simple,” he said, because it will be solely tailored to lift five Bluebird satellites into low-Earth orbit at a time. It will have 20 metric tons of lift capacity, a reusable first stage, and launch twice a month from a pad at Wallops Flight Facility in Virginia that formerly served the Antares rocket. It will have all composite tanks and three stages. And, Avellan said, it will be ready to launch in 2028.

If that timeline sounds preposterous, that’s because it is.

However, Avellan believes AST has a workaround: It is committed to spending $1.3 billion to acquire enough engines for the first three Blacklift launches from Firefly Aerospace, as well as a license to manufacture additional engines over time. He is basing this argument on the Miranda first stage engine, which he said is “ready to fly.”

But that’s not quite true. However, Miranda has not yet completed its formal qualification campaign, much less flown on an orbital rocket. Firefly chief executive Jason Kim said this week that qualification testing remains underway. Moreover, little is known publicly about the readiness of the upper stage engine, Vira, that AST is also acquiring from Firefly for its rocket.

Another questionable assertion is Avellan’s statement that 80 percent of a rocket’s design cycle is due to engine development. “I mean, that has been proven,” he said.

I’m not sure it has. Propulsion is definitely one of the most challenging aspects of developing a rocket.

However, Rocket Lab, which has considerably more experience building orbital rockets than AST, has suffered two significant setbacks this year involving Neutron’s composite propellant tanks. These structures, as well as flight software, avionics, stage separation, payload fairings, qualification testing, and so much more, can lead to significant delays in the development of a rocket. And then there are the equally difficult challenges of scaling from development to production at rate, and recovering the first stage.

Avellan and Scott Wisniewski, president of AST SpaceMobile, also did not get into specifics about financing the development of Blacklift. The company has already invested more than $1 billion in engines and will likely need billions more to develop and scale the vehicle.

Wisniewski said AST SpaceMobile plans to spend “several hundred million dollars” in 2027 on Blacklift development, but it is not clear whether that includes milestone payments to Firefly for propulsion. According to the company’s balance sheet, it had about $2.8 billion in unrestricted cash and cash equivalents at the end of September, and it has historically been successful in raising additional capital.

The company’s underlying business plan makes sense. There is a potentially very large market for space-to-ground connectivity for mobile devices. Traditional mobile service operators are growing wary about working with SpaceX, viewing the company as a competitor rather than a partner. And the recent issues with Starlink in Ukraine may lead some nations to seek alternative partners for these services.

“First of all, I’m no Musk, and I don’t want to be,” Avellan said on Thursday. “We partner. We don’t compete. We enhance. … Why? Because we partner with them. We don’t compete with them. We don’t insult them. You know, we partner with them.”

SpaceX went from being a rocket company to operating satellites. Now Avellan and AST seek to go from being a satellite operator to launching rockets. Consider that SpaceX took eight years from its founding to launch the Falcon 9, another five years to successfully land a booster, and several more years to achieve a high flight rate. AST is seeking to compress much of that process into approximately two years.

But rocket science is in no way simple, and the road ahead is sure to be rocky. Avellan said AST doesn’t really want to be in the launch business. Given how difficult, demanding, and expensive the launch business is, he may get his wish in a few years.

#305. Here are the 25 original Xbox games you can now play on PC

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Microsoft’s adding achievements to original Xbox games for the first time, too.

Microsoft’s adding achievements to original Xbox games for the first time, too.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

For the 25th anniversary of Xbox, Microsoft is beginning to make good on its paid “next-gen game preservation” promise. Starting today, you can play 25 different original Xbox games on your Windows PC, as long as you have (or are willing to pay) for digital copies.

In July, Microsoft originally launched the idea with just four Xbox games, so 25 is progress, and Microsoft says all these games are now playable with keyboard and mouse.

Plus, Microsoft says it’s adding achievements to original Xbox games for the first time, starting with four games: Star Wars: Knights of the Old Republic and its sequel, Blinx: The Time Sweeper, and Conker: Live and Reloaded. You can unlock achievements playing them on Xbox One and Xbox Series consoles as well, after a system update. More games should get them in future.

Here’s the full list of Xbox on PC games, with the 21 new additions in bold:

Yes, that’s a lot of Star Wars games, most of which already have a PC version, and few games that were exclusive to Xbox at the time. (But many of those Star Wars games were excellent.)

You also won’t be able to use an original Xbox disc to play these games, I’m afraid, as original Xbox (and Xbox 360) discs aren’t part of Microsoft’s disc-to-digital program; they predate the unique serial number that Microsoft embedded into Xbox One discs. In case you’re wondering, Xbox on PC backward compatibility appears to be powered by an emulator.

Also, most of the new games aren’t currently included with Xbox Game Pass, according to Microsoft; just Blinx, Conker, Crimson Skies, Fusion Frenzy, Morrowind, and Grabbed by the Ghoulies.

Leaked Microsoft documents previously showed us that October 2026 would be the “full launch” of original Xbox games on PC, while Xbox 360 games would have a “gradual” rollout to next-gen consoles between 2027 and 2028, which are when Microsoft is expected to roll out a handheld and a Helix box respectively. The way things are shaping up, the Xbox Helix should play games from every generation of Xbox, even if Helix fundamentally turns out to be a Windows PC.

#304. Najib Razak, Ex-Prime Minister of Malaysia, Is Released to House Arrest

Najib Razak, who was brought down in the multibillion-dollar 1MDB scandal, was released from prison to serve the rest of his sentence in his mansion.

Zunaira Saieed reported from Kuala Lumpur, Malaysia, and Richard C. Paddock from Bangkok.

Malaysia’s disgraced former prime minister, Najib Razak, whose role in the colossal theft of public funds earned him the nickname “Man of Steal,” was released from prison on Tuesday. He was let out under a royal pardon that allows him to serve the remainder of his six-year sentence at the mansion he calls home.

Mr. Najib, 73, who has spent the past four years in prison on corruption charges, was delivered on Tuesday night to his home in one of the most exclusive neighborhoods of the capital, Kuala Lumpur. The entire 49,000-square-foot property was officially declared a federal prison on Friday in preparation for his homecoming.

Voters ousted Mr. Najib, the son of one prime minister and the nephew of another, in 2018. He was later convicted of graft, after $4.5 billion disappeared from 1Malaysia Development Berhad, the state investment fund that he founded and oversaw. The scandal brought down his government, but subsequent royal pardons and his move to house arrest have raised accusations of special treatment for elites.

“It is an absolute mockery of justice,” Suresh Kumar, a property manager in Kuala Lumpur. “The law works for the rich and powerful and not for the poor.”

Mr. Najib arrived at his mansion about 9:30 p.m. in a silver Proton, a Malaysian-made SUV, accompanied by nine other cars and two motorcycles. Dozens of reporters who had gathered to witness his return were kept at bay by police officers who had blocked off the street near his house.

Mr. Najib is expected to remain under house arrest until August 2028. His legal problems, however, continue. He is due to begin serving a 15-year sentence on separate corruption charges related to the scandal when his current term ends. The High Court of Malaysia also ordered him to pay a staggering $2.8 billion fine. He is appealing that conviction.

#303. Netflix releases new trailer for Ray Gunn

“It’s hard being the only honest players in a crooked game.”

We caught the first trailer for the upcoming Netflix animated film Ray Gunn in September and were charmed by director Brad Bird’s blend of a Jetsons-like future with classic 1930s noir detective thrillers. Bird has described it as a mashup of The Maltese Falcon and Buck Rogers, and the latest trailer definitely gives off that energy.

As previously reported, Bird has directed some of our all-time favorite animated films: The Iron Giant (1999), The Incredibles (2004), and Ratatouille (2007), with the latter two snagging well-deserved Oscar wins. He’s been kicking around the idea for Ray Gunn for the last 30 years, working on his many other film projects while the Ray Gunn script—co-written by Matthew Robbins, who also co-wrote 1987’s Batteries Not Included with Bird—“stayed in a filing cabinet at Warner Brothers,” the director said earlier this year.

The film’s inspiration came when Bird heard the opening of the B-52s’ “Planet Claire” (a personal favorite that also provides the soundtrack to the new trailer) and initially thought it was a cover of Henry Mancini’s theme music from the classic TV show Peter Gunn. Bird is a longtime fan of film noir and hard-boiled detectives, and he came up with the idea of a futuristic detective named Ray Gunn (a play on “raygun,” of course). “It’s film noir and a hard-boiled detective story, but it’s got a lot more comedy and a lot more action than the average film noir,” said Bird.

Per the official premise: “In Metropia, a gigantic city in an alternate future as seen from 1939, private eye Raymond Gunn (voiced by Sam Rockwell) is drawn into a case involving aliens, murder, and a multimedia star named Venus Nova (voiced by Scarlett Johansson).”

Unlike Bird’s Pixar fare, Ray Gunn targets a slightly older demographic with more mature themes.

In addition to Rockwell and Johansson, the all-star voice cast includes Tom Waits as Ray’s one-eyed alien colleague, Eyera (pronounced Ira); Matt Berry as alien mob boss Francis “Sluggy” Xavier; Bobby Cannavale as Venus’ husband, who hires Ray to spy on his wife; Alan Tudyk as Venus’ manager, Barry Vogel; Michael Chernus as a studio fixer named Sam; and Patton Oswalt as a hologram. Bird provides the voices for Xavier’s alien henchmen, Gunsol and Musker. Kathryn Hunter, John Ratzenberger, and Jamie Costa will also voice as-yet-undisclosed characters.

Ray Gunn releases on Netflix on December 18, 2026—the same day both Avengers: Doomsday and Dune: Part 3 hit theaters, so it will be alternative family fare for those disinclined to brave the holiday theatrical crowds. The film will also screen in select theaters on December 4, 2026.

After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Representative Thomas Massie, a Kentucky Republican whose libertarian leanings and independent streak have made him a target of President Trump’s, has brought impeachment charges against Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth.

It could be one of his last major acts in Congress: The 55-year-old Mr. Massie, a seven-term representative, is retiring at the end of his term after losing a primary to a challenger backed by the president.

#299. Google faces calls for AI moratoriums in schools after Gemini pilot backlash

Families confront Google after teen’s lawsuit labels Gemini a “suicide coach.”

CHICAGO—Families joined protests in seven cities nationwide on Wednesday, rallying against what they see as Google’s bid to entrench its AI models in schools, turn kids of all ages into Gemini guinea pigs, and hook them on AI.

The backlash follows a Google statement last week, confirming that there will be no way for students to use Google Search without AI. While Google claimed it was giving schools “more choice, not less”—by turning off Search, Gemini, and Gemini Notebook by default—it would not provide an option that allowed Search without AI. For students, the only way to avoid interacting with AI was to remember to add “-ai” to every query, parents worried.

In Chicago, many families showed up to protest Google, despite cold, rainy weather. Waving signs, dozens of protesters marched up to Google’s downtown office as workers seemed to be leaving for the day, shouting chants like “Hey Google, nice try, we don’t want your Gemini” and “Hey, Google, leave our kids alone!”

Called to action by a nonprofit grassroots advocacy group, the Illinois Families for Public Schools (ILFPS), some parents and students had previously won a major AI fight. In August, the group pressured Chicago Public Schools (CPS) to pull out of a Gemini pilot that would have roped in all high school students districtwide—without consulting parents. And now, ILFPS is demanding a moratorium on AI until risks to students are better understood and CPS can draft a policy to protect students from potential learning setbacks or more harmful outcomes from engaging Google’s AI bots. If the group wins, it will join cities like Los Angeles and New York City in cautiously banning AI in schools.

Most alarming to parents, Google now faces a lawsuit from a 13-year-old student in Durham, North Carolina named “Emma Doe.” As Emma has alleged, she was a happy, well-adjusted, straight-A student when she started using Gemini on her school-issued Chromebook. Emma’s parents had no idea she was using Gemini, as the lawsuit alleged that Google “deliberately” kept them in the dark while driving Emma into so-called “AI psychosis.” Over time, normal school-related interactions with the chatbot led to more intense chats, and ultimately the chatbot allegedly coached Emma to plan her own suicide and funeral. The school warned her parents and averted the danger after an internal system flagged a chat labeled “Funeral,” but Emma’s story stoked fears of parents joining protests not just in Durham, but also in Boston; Boulder, Colorado; Chicago; Los Angeles; New York City; and Portland, Oregon.

Emma’s lawsuit accuses Google of exploiting its dominant position in education technology and “sneaking” Gemini into Chromebooks, while falsely marketing them as safe.

“As a direct consequence of Google’s choices, Emma was baited by its defectively designed products into repeated, unmonitored, sycophantic AI chatbot interactions on her school Chromebook without her parents ever knowing. And, but for a fortunate turn of events, Google’s AI may well have manipulated Emma into taking her own life,” the lawsuit alleged. “Google must be held to account for treating the lives of children as disposable in service of its bottom line.”

Google has promised to “continue to engage deeply with educators to ensure we are giving schools more choice, more controls, and improved Search for learning.” However, Google’s statements so far attempting to address parents’ concerns focus exclusively on documented harms to learning. On that front, Google claims that it’s solving the problem of AI eroding students’ critical thinking skills by launching yet another pilot for students. In this pilot, students using AI Mode and AI Overviews will be offered a “step-by-step guided learning through problems, so that students don’t get quick answers to schoolwork,” Google said.

Cassie Creswell, executive director of ILFPS, told Ars that she’s been an advocate monitoring edtech issues for more than a decade. And even she was blindsided by Google’s AI moves.

“I am aware of AI Overviews when you search, which have a whole slew of problems, but I did not know that you really actually just have an interface where you can interact with Gemini in the chatbot way on everyone’s Chromebook still in Chicago public schools,” Creswell said. “And that’s so disturbing.”

She acknowledged that kids encounter AI on their phones and shared that her daughter said her friends like using ChatGPT better than Gemini. But she said that the fact that some kids use AI on personal devices doesn’t mean that schools should be encouraging adoption at young ages when kids’ brains are still developing, and risks remain largely unknown.

“There’s all kinds of dangerous things out in the world, but we don’t have our school district handing it to kids at their desk,” Creswell said.

Meanwhile, schools seem to be part of the transparency problem.

Creswell said that currently, the Chicago school district “is telling us their official line is that kids don’t have access to generative AI tools and that we don’t need a district-level policy on generative AI because they have guidebooks.” However, she’s reviewed Google’s CPS contracts, and they suggest that kids are interacting with generative AI and frontier models. The ongoing confusion has led parents to demand “a real policy” that the CPS “board votes on and that has the input of teachers, parents, students, and experts who are not being funded by the tech industry.”

Creswell told Ars that parents are particularly upset that Google is not transparent about how it processes and uses student data. She’s exhaustively researched Google contracts through Freedom of Information Act requests and told Ars that dozens of agreements she reviewed offer no indication of how CPS partners like Google—as well as Anthropic and OpenAI—are handling student data. Although she understands that “it’s probably just an honest statement of fact” that Google has built AI inextricably into Search, parents have a right to know how their children’s data is ingested into Google’s models, she said.

“Any sort of transparency on what is actually happening in terms of the data that they collect and what they do with it, that is absolutely missing,” Creswell said. And “it’s very unclear what data is being saved.”

To Creswell and other advocates, it seems likely that kids’ data is collected no matter what, since “even if you do ‘-AI,’ and you don’t get a text summary, your search data and what’s being returned is all basically inside the generative AI model.” Although Illinois law only allows commercial use of de-identified data, parents think Google should clearly explain what steps it takes to ensure data can’t be recombined to re-identify kids.

Ars reached out to Google to see if the company could clarify if Creswell’s read is accurate or if there’s any meaningful distinction between adding “-AI” to an individual query versus building a button that would automatically add “-AI” to every query. Google did not clarify, instead emphasizing that schools will control whether students can access the Gemini app, Gemini Notebook, and Search, with options to toggle each off individually, but no option to use Search without AI.

Currently, that policy specifies that “we receive customer data through the Core Services and process it according to the school’s (the customer’s) instructions. This customer data includes anything submitted, stored, sent, or received through Core Services by you or your school.” Google collects that data on that activity, as well as account information “like name, email address and password” and location information, the policy said.

The policy didn’t discuss Gemini but noted that data from using core services helps maintain services, improve personalization, and increase security. And “the data we collect in the Additional Services is used across our services to deliver, maintain, and improve our services; develop new services; provide personalized services; measure performance; communicate with you; and protect Google, our users, and the public.”

To parents, the policy provides no reassurance that their kids’ data isn’t part of Google’s plan to dominate the AI industry.

“We should not have any child data being collected in a school setting being used for commercial purposes,” Creswell said.

In Chicago, a recently re-elected CPS school board member, Karen Zaccor, is pushing for an AI moratorium in schools after talking with city and community colleges about an allegedly noticeable decline in reading, math, and writing skills from students graduating from CPS schools. In Emma’s case, Google allegedly suppressed chat logs, and the school withheld safety logs, frustrating parents who already viewed AI as more harmful than beneficial in education.

At the Google protest Wednesday, parents waved signs that threatened to eject Google from schools, with slogans like “No Google + No AI = Win/Win.” Many kids used favorite cartoon characters from shows like Beavis and Butthead and South Park to slam Google’s seemingly cynical approach to introducing AI in schools. One young girl wearing pink rain boots held up a cardboard sign, declaring in colorful markers, “I like to read!!”

At its peak, about 60 people stood outside Google’s downtown Chicago office, enduring the rain and listening to teachers and students who oppose reckless AI adoption.

One particularly moving speech came from a CPS student, Reese Mackey, who spoke for students. She challenged Google to more carefully weigh the long-term harms of aggressively pushing AI onto students by claiming they’ll be left behind and struggle to find jobs without AI skills. Creswell told Ars the opposite seems true, as Emma Doe’s lawsuit pointed to research branding Gemini as “the most dangerous marketed for use by youth” and “confirming the existence of AI-induced psychosis.”

“What about the future of our youth?” Mackey said. “What about the future of our minds?”

After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#298. Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out the New York Times

Some VIP Twitter users woke up on Saturday expecting to have lost their coveted blue verification check marks in a previously announced purge by Elon Musk. Instead, Twitter appeared to target a single account from a major publication Musk dislikes and changed the language on its site in a way that obscures why users are verified.

Twitter had said it would “begin winding down” blue checks granted under its old verification system — which emphasized protecting high-profile users at risk of impersonation — on April 1. In order to stay verified, Musk said, users would have to pay $8 per month to join the platform’s Twitter Blue subscription service, which has allowed accounts to pay for verification since December.

But one high-profile account did lose its blue check over the weekend: the main account for the New York Times, which had previously told CNN it would not pay for verification.

William Shatner is mad he may have to pay to keep his blue Twitter checkmark. Elon Musk says too bad

After an account that often engages with Musk posted a meme this weekend about the Times declining to pay for verification, Musk responded in a tweet saying, “Oh ok, we’ll take it off then.” Musk then lashed out at the Times — just the latest instance of the billionaire slamming journalists or media outlets — in a series of tweets that claimed the outlet’s coverage is boring and “propaganda.”

The weekend moves are just the latest example of Twitter creating confusion and whiplash for users over feature changes — and in this case, not just any users, but many of the most high-profile accounts that have long been a key selling point for the platform. It also highlights how Musk often appears to guide decisions about the platform more by whims than by policy.

Although the New York Times’ main account lost its blue check, its other accounts, such as those for its arts, travel and books content, remained verified. (It’s not clear why the New York Times doesn’t have a gold “organizations” check mark, like accounts for other news outlets, including the Associated Press and the Washington Post.) After its blue check was removed, a spokesperson for the New York Times reiterated to CNN that it does not plan to pay for verification.

Twitter, which laid off most of its public relations staff last fall, did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

In a separate puzzling move, Twitter’s blue bird logo at the top of the site was replaced on Monday with doge, the meme representing the cryptocurrency dogecoin, which Musk has promoted. The price of dogecoin shot up 20% on Monday.

Musk has been threatening to take away “legacy” blue check marks from users verified under Twitter’s old system since shortly after he bought Twitter last fall.

In early November, Twitter launched the option for people paying for its Twitter Blue subscription service to receive blue checks. The program was quickly put on pause after being plagued by a wave of celebrity and corporate impersonators, and was relaunched in December.

Twitter also rolled out a color-coded verification system with differently colored marks for companies and government entities, but Musk continued to say that individual users would eventually have to pay for blue checks.

In the days leading up to the blue check purge that wasn’t, prominent users such as actor William Shatner and anti-bullying activist Monica Lewinksy pushed back against the idea that, as power users that draw attention to the site, they should have to pay for a feature that keeps them safe from impersonation.

By muddying the reason accounts are verified, the new label could risk making it easier for people to scam or impersonate high-profile users. Experts in inauthentic behavior have also said it’s not clear that reserving verification for paid users will reduce the number of bots on the site, an issue Musk has raised on and off over the past year.

Musk, for his part, has previously presented changes to Twitter’s verification system as a way of “treating everyone equally.”

“There shouldn’t be a different standard for celebrities,” he said in a tweet last week. The paid feature could also drive revenue, which could help Musk, who is on the hook for significant debt after buying Twitter for $44 billion.

Musk last week also said that starting on April 15, only verified accounts would be recommended in users’ “For You” feeds alongside the accounts they follow.

–CNN’s Oliver Darcy contributed to this report.

#295. Meghan Markle Makes First Public Appearance Since Her Father’s Arrest

The Duchess of Sussex accompanied her husband, Prince Harry, in Brussels at an event about online safety for children.

Meghan, the Duchess of Sussex, made a public appearance in Brussels on Tuesday, the first since her father was arrested on charges related to the possession of child sexual abuse images.

Meghan was with her husband, Prince Harry, who spoke at the European Parliament for an event about digital safety and child protection. Meghan did not speak at the event, though she had planned to introduce Harry, according to Liam Maguire, a spokesman for the duchess. Mr. Maguire declined to comment further.

Thomas Markle Sr., who is estranged from Meghan, was arrested in Florida on Friday. According to the U.S. attorney’s office in Los Angeles, the arrest came after law enforcement officials discovered sexually explicit photographs of him with prepubescent girls in two Los Angeles-area storage units that he had rented for nearly two decades.

Last month, before Mr. Markle’s arrest, Meghan said she was appalled by the allegations surrounding him, which she said helped explain why she had kept her children away from him. Meghan first learned of allegations against her father around eight years ago when a letter was sent to the unit of London’s Metropolitan Police that was at the time responsible for her security, she said.

Harry and Meghan, and their two children, moved back to Britain from California in August. Since then, the duke and duchess have largely laid low with relatively few public appearances.

#294. ‘It’s a mistake’: reactions from tech leaders on Jensen Huang’s resignation

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Many prominent voices in technology and the president of the US have publicly supported the former Nvidia CEO despite his ouster.﻿

Many prominent voices in technology and the president of the US have publicly supported the former Nvidia CEO despite his ouster.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Nvidia announced this morning that Jensen Huang has resigned as CEO following reports of two affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy and one with a direct report. The situation surrounding Huang has unfolded quickly, with The New York Times only reporting on Friday that Huang and the suspected spy were “close” before other dominoes started to fall, leading to a swift fall from grace for one of the biggest names in technology. (Hours after Huang’s resignation was announced, Reuters reported that he also had an “intimate relationship” with another Nvidia employee.)

Prominent voices in technology have come out in support of Huang, with some calling the resignation a “waste” and a sad day for “all humanity.” The narrative among those leaders on X, at least, is that Huang’s resignation was rushed — perhaps an overreaction to salacious news stories about his personal life. Some others have had a more neutral reaction, commenting more on Huang’s achievements at the company’s helm. You can see a collection of those responses below.

“NOW THAT Nvidia CEO JENSEN HUANG, who has done a fantastic job, has resigned, there is only one man to take his place - TIM COOK of Apple. A Legend replaces a Legend!”, Trump said on Truth Social.

Elon Musk said in a reply to an X user that it would “be an honor” to hire Huang. He also replied to a post that said “You’d think we’d have learned from Steve Jobs and Travis Kalanick that one does not just simply replace the founder…” by saying “True words.”

Sweeney said that today is “A sad day for NVIDIA, developers, America, and all humanity. Jensen founded and ran one of the few companies to reach a massive scale while driving massive continued innovation and ubiquity, while others fell into the trap of ever-increasing extraction.”

Ahead of Huang’s resignation, Sweeney posted about how Huang “saved” a GDC 2015 presentation by dropping off a graphics card and said that “The dude truly is the visionary driver of the future of tech from the gaming GPU era to CUDA to AI supercomputers.”

“China wants Jensen out, so I want him in,” Luckey said last night, before Huang’s resignation was announced.

Mullenweg reposted an X post that followed his brief September ouster as CEO: “Founder advice: If you don’t have a coup attempt every few years, you’re not hiring strong enough leaders.” He said, “Just putting this back out there, for no reason whatsoever.”

“What a waste,” Hansson said this morning, replying to his own post from hours before: “NVIDIA would be wrong to fire Jensen. His marital indiscretions are between him, his wife, and a reprimand from the board. Have some proportionality.”

“This whole thing sucks, and I wish none of it had happened,” Delangue said. “It’s a really sad day! But I want to make one thing clear: we’ll keep fighting for open-source AI, harder than ever. This is too important to slow down now. Let’s go!

“It’s a mistake for Nvidia to let Jensen resign,” Zhao said.

“If that Spy’s goal was to destabilize one of the most successful American companies, they achieved their goal,” Steinberger said. “Ousting Jensen feels rushed and not right.” (“I agree,” replied Sweeney.)

Srinivas thanked Huang for “33 years of bold leadership” in a post this morning. “You built a small startup into America’s most valuable company, with relentless improvement. Your grit and perseverance should always be remembered. Your personal generosity with time and advice to founders like me has secured a bright future for America and AI.”

Calacanis reposted Trump’s post, tagging Tim Cook and telling him to “Keep the seat warm” for Huang while “the investigation is sorted out” and saying that “America needs you Mr. Cook.” Last night, he also said that Nvidia shareholders should “decide Jensen’s fate after the investigation is complete — not the press or online mobs.”

In a post talking about how he has seen Huang show “kindness, humility, care to everyone around him,” Fridman said that “I’m in shock frankly at all of this, including the mass hysteria around it. I love America, and I think NVIDIA letting Jensen go is terrible for America. I stand with Jensen.”
