#198. Lots of Competition to Catholicism in Peru as Pope Returns for Visit

Pope Leo spent nearly two decades in Peru, rising to bishop and becoming a citizen. But many Peruvians have turned to evangelical churches, seeking a more expressive and direct connection to God.

Pope Leo spent nearly two decades in Peru, rising to bishop and becoming a citizen. But many Peruvians have turned to evangelical churches, seeking a more expressive and direct connection to God.

Members of Agua Viva, a Pentecostal megachurch in Lima, Peru, at a prayer concert on Sunday.Credit...

Strobe lights scanned a crowd of outstretched arms as a rock band took the stage, smoke billowing out and applause echoing through the coliseum. The pastor bounced out to the beat of a drum roll, a microphone curved around his face.

This Sunday’s sermon, Pastor Sergio Hornung announced, would be about “the territory still to conquer.”

“There are many places where they talk to you about God,” said Scarlet Reyes, 21, a former Catholic who started coming to this Pentecostal church, Agua Viva, two years ago. “But here, they teach you how to know God.”

Pope Leo arrives on Wednesday in Peru, where he served as a priest for two decades, rose to bishop and became a Peruvian citizen. He returns at a time when the church faces two major challenges in Latin America: growing secularization and competition from other Christian denominations.

While Catholicism remains the dominant religious force in the country and the region, evangelical churches have made enormous gains, with congregants saying these churches offer more direct relationships with God and a more expressive, modern style of worship.

#197. The unfolding Jensen Huang scandal: a timeline

Nvidia co-founder and now former CEO Jensen Huang has never been as flashy or controversy-prone as AI leaders like OpenAI’s Sam Altman or SpaceXAI’s Elon Musk. Now, the 63-year-old industry kingpin has admitted that he cheated on his wife of 40 years in affairs that could have ramifications beyond Huang’s personal life and U.S. national security.

U.S. officials believe that one of Huang’s likely romantic interests, Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, is a Chinese spy who has been feeding information back to Beijing. (Alice Huang, a Chinese citizen, is of no relation to Jensen Huang.)

Megaspeed is a Singapore-based AI infrastructure company that reportedly purchased $4.8 billion in Nvidia chips over the last two years. The U.S. and Singapore have both previously investigated Megaspeed over suspicions that the firm was illegally sharing chips with China. The Justice Department has since taken over the federal investigation into the company, The New York Times has reported.

The story took a sudden new turn on Wednesday. Citing five people who worked at Nvidia, the Times reported that Huang also had an extramarital relationship with Kimberly Powell, the company’s vice president of healthcare, who reports directly to him. Nvidia, Huang, and Powell have not confirmed the report. Reached by a Times reporter, Huang reportedly said, “I don’t think you should be talking to me directly,” before hanging up.

By early Thursday morning, Huang was no longer CEO of the company he co-founded 33 years ago. Then, Thursday afternoon, Reuters dropped another bombshell, publishing a report alleging that Huang had yet another extramarital affair with another unnamed Nvidia employee.

The U.S. and China are embroiled in a fierce technological arms race to build the most advanced AI infrastructure, which could shape each country’s economic future, military prowess, and capacity for innovation. Nvidia is valuable in this competition as one of the world’s largest makers of AI chips, which are essential for accelerating AI development. But as an American company, Nvidia faces government restrictions on the sale of its most powerful chips to Chinese entities.

As the controversy unfolds, we are organizing the most relevant events into a reverse-chronological timeline to keep you up to date.

After Huang was already ousted from Nvidia, we continued to learn more incriminating claims about the former CEO. According to Reuters, Huang had a third affair with an additional, unnamed employee of Nvidia.

Nvidia confirmed in an SEC filing on Thursday that the board accepted Huang’s resignation on Wednesday. The company’s current CFO, Colette Kress, will take over as interim CEO while still fulfilling her CFO duties. Nvidia is expected to conduct a larger search to determine a permanent successor to Huang.

Harvey C. Jones, a managing partner of Square Wave Ventures and former CEO of Synopsys, was appointed as the company’s first-ever chair of the board of directors. Jones has been on Nvidia’s board since 1993.

Later in the day, Huang confirmed that he would testify in Congress at some point before Thanksgiving, which falls on November 26.

“Jensen and I have been very at odds as some of you already know on export controls, but Nvidia is a very important company to the United States of America,” House Foreign Affairs Committee Chairman Brian Mast said to reporters. “I don’t want to see anything hurt in Nvidia, but he has some very serious questions to answer.”

The testimony is expected to take place behind closed doors, but Mast believes the briefing will be recorded and transcribed.

After reports of a second extramarital affair, Nvidia’s board reportedly plans to meet to discuss Huang’s future at the company, per The Wall Street Journal. Stock fell about 8% in after-hours trading on Wednesday and is down 11% in the past five days.

Huang famously delegates very little and has about 60 direct reports, he has said. The company has not had an obvious successor or a succession plan.

The New York Times reported that Jensen Huang also had an affair with Nvidia vice president of healthcare Kimberly Powell, who reports directly to the CEO.

According to the report, Huang and Powell were captured on film “being affectionate” at a hotel during a conference earlier this year.

Huang has not responded to a request from three Republican congressmen to appear for a closed-door briefing for questioning about his affair with an alleged Chinese spy. House China Committee Chairman John Moolenaar said Wednesday on Fox Business that if Huang doesn’t agree to the briefing, he’ll likely be subpoenaed.

The Nvidia CEO pulled out of upcoming speaking engagements at the ElevenLabs Summit and Anthropic CEO Forum.

Alice Huang and her family reportedly built a network of companies in China and Singapore to acquire Nvidia chips, including founding a Chinese company called Shanghai Century Litong. Founded in 2023, it became an Nvidia-preferred cloud partner, giving it priority access to chips, The Information reported. The Information also reported that Shanghai Century Litong facilitated buying $7.5 billion worth of Nvidia’s chips, including banned chips.

Nvidia’s head lawyer, Tim Teter, intervened in a 2025 U.S. government probe surrounding the suspected smuggling of AI chips by Megaspeed International, Bloomberg reported. Teter contacted Commerce Under Secretary Jeffrey Kessler asking questions about whether the Trump administration played a role in detaining Alice Huang.

In early 2025, Alice Huang and a business partner sought China-based investors for an Asian data center, promising they had no problem in securing GPUs, the Financial Times reported.

On Sunday night, President Trump weighed in on Huang’s controversy and showed support for the Nvidia CEO.

“I have not spoken to him about it. I just know he’s a fine person, but you never know what’s going on,” Trump said.

When asked if Huang should remain chief of Nvidia, Trump said, “I think so. Oh yeah, sure.”

Jensen Huang wrote in a letter to Nvidia employees — which was also published as a press release — that he cheated on his wife.

“I owe my family and all of you an apology,” Huang told his company. “I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry.”

Huang did not mention Alice Huang by name in the message, but linked his infidelity to “the national security concerns raised in news reports.”

“Nvidia’s Board of Directors has initiated an independent investigation. I support the investigation and will cooperate fully. I love our country and our company. I am committed to protecting Nvidia and supporting America’s national security. I am proud and grateful to be an American,” Huang wrote.

Nvidia shares slid over 5% the following day.

Three Republican congressmen — Chairman John Moolenaar of the House Select Committee on China, Chairman Brian Mast of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, and Chairman Rick Crawford of the House Permanent Select Committee on Intelligence — sent a request to Huang for a closed-door joint briefing with their three committees.

“Recent reports raise significant national security concerns regarding the [People’s Republic of China]’s access to sensitive U.S. technology and information,” the chairmen wrote. “The issue of PRC infiltration and access to cutting-edge U.S. technology is core to the investigative work of our three committees this Congress. It is vital that Congress determines the extent to which U.S. national security has been placed at risk.”

U.S. officials had been investigating Alice Huang and Megaspeed for more than a year when the New York Times broke the story in late October that the two executives also had a close relationship.

An Nvidia spokesperson denied that this relationship was something out of the ordinary.

“Jensen’s interactions with Ms. Huang are not unusual or unexpected. Meetings with customers are part of a CEO’s job; Jensen talks with thousands of customer executives every year,” Nvidia spokesperson John Rizzo said. He added that “if the U.S. government believes that any person is a threat to American industry, it would let us know.”

U.S. officials circulated a secret memo among policymakers that warned that Megaspeed’s Alice Huang, who had a relationship with Jensen Huang, was a spy for China’s Ministry of State Security. This memo was not public knowledge until it was later reported by the New York Times.

Secretary of State Marco Rubio supervised the drafting of the memo.

Around the same time, per the New York Times’ report, U.S. President Donald Trump was briefed about intelligence findings that possible Chinese spy Alice Huang had formed a close relationship with Jensen Huang. Trump did not act on this information.

The Nvidia CEO, an ally of Trump, has repeatedly advocated for the U.S. government to let Nvidia expand its business in China.

Megaspeed was confirmed to be under investigation by both the U.S. and Singaporean governments in October 2025. As a customer of Nvidia, Megaspeed was suspected of helping Chinese companies get around American embargoes on chip exports.

Megaspeed, a neocloud that used Nvidia chips, had been renting computing power to Chinese internet giant Alibaba and other Chinese companies, the Times reported. A Commerce Department official who had visited Megaspeed’s data center in Malaysia in 2024 had found a hoard of Nvidia equipment that was still packed in boxes and the concern was that this equipment was being directly shipped somewhere else, like China, rather than installed and sold as a cloud service.

Jensen Huang had been spotted socializing with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang as early as 2024 in Taiwan, where the Nvidia CEO was born.

These investigations into Megaspeed highlighted concerns about the degree to which Nvidia is able to track its chip exports. At the time, an Nvidia spokesperson told press that the company did not have “any reason to believe products had been diverted.”

“NVIDIA visited multiple Megaspeed sites yet again earlier this week and confirmed what we previously observed — Megaspeed is running a small commercial cloud, like many other companies throughout the world, as allowed by U.S. export control rules,” the Nvidia spokesperson said at the time.

#195. Micron Technology: China probes US chip maker for cybersecurity risks as tech tension escalates

China has launched a cybersecurity probe into Micron Technology, one of America’s largest memory chip makers, in apparent retaliation after US allies in Asia and Europe announced new restrictions on the sale of key technology to Beijing.

The Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC) will review products sold by Micron in the country, according to a statement by the watchdog late on Friday.

The move is aimed at “ensuring the security of key information infrastructure supply chains, preventing cybersecurity risks caused by hidden product problems, and maintaining national security,” it noted.

It came on the same day that Japan, a US ally, said it would restrict the export of advanced chip manufacturing equipment to countries including China, following similar moves by the United States and the Netherlands.

Washington and its allies have announced curbs on China’s semiconductor industry, which strike at the heart of Beijing’s bid to become a tech superpower.

Last month, the Netherlands also unveiled new restrictions on overseas sales of semiconductor technology, citing the need to protect national security. In October, the United States banned Chinese companies from buying advanced chips and chipmaking equipment without a license.

Micron told CNN it was aware of the review.

“We are in communication with the CAC and are cooperating fully,” it said, adding that it stands by the security of its products. “Micron’s product shipments, engineering, manufacturing, sales and other functions are operating as normal.”

Shares in Micron sank 4.4% on Wall Street Friday following the news, the biggest drop in more than three months. On Monday, they closed another 1.2% lower. Micron derives more than 10% of its revenue from China.

In an earlier filing, the Idaho-based company had warned of such risks.

“The Chinese government may restrict us from participating in the China market or may prevent us from competing effectively with Chinese companies,” it said last week.

Japan joins the US and Europe in chipmaking curbs on China

China has strongly criticized restrictions on tech exports, saying last month it “firmly opposes” such measures.

In efforts to boost growth and job creation, Beijing is seeking to woo foreign investments as it grapples with mounting economic challenges. The newly minted premier Li Qiang and several top economic officials have been rolling out the welcome wagon for global CEOs and promising they would “provide a good environment and services.”

But Beijing has also exerted growing pressure on foreign companies to bring them into line with its agenda.

Last month, authorities closed the Beijing office of Mintz Group, a US corporate intelligence firm, and detained five local staff.

Days earlier, they suspended Deloitte’s operations in Beijing for three months and imposed a fine of $31 million over alleged lapses in its work auditing a state-owned distressed debt manager.

CNN’s Michelle Toh, Junko Ogura, Sandi Sidhu and Chris Lau contributed to the article.

#192. Muse can now place calls to US businesses — if you pay

Meta’s Muse, which now has over 10 million users, the company said last month, has been working to make consumer AI a mainstream product for everyday users. Placing calls is one of the ways it aims to get there, as it could handle another task on people’s to-do lists.

Notes Alexandr Wang, Meta’s chief AI officer and head of its Superintelligence Labs division, the feature could be used to take care of calls people don’t want to make, like those to their cable company, the DMV, or to a restaurant that doesn’t offer online reservations.

🚨MUSE FEATURE ALERT!!!🚨lots of you have been asking for this one…muse can make phone calls for you! ☎️📞📲let muse take care of all those calls you don’t want to make. insurance? cable? DMV? dinner res? not your problem anymore.out now to paid subs in the US 🇺🇸 pic.twitter.com/dsyMNmI2Ub

Previously, only a select number of Muse early adopters were able to try this feature after asking the AI for access. Now, anyone who pays for Muse can make this type of request. Currently, Muse subscription plans start at $20 per month for 500 million Muse tokens per week. Heavier AI users can opt to pay $100 per month for 3 billion tokens.

Muse is not the only AI agent able to place calls to businesses; the startup Instinct has been offering a similar option, as has Google’s Gemini. However, while the AI tools are now certainly capable of handling the task, the reception on the other end of the line can be mixed. Many businesses and receptionists hang up on calls from AI agents as soon as they identify that it’s not a human on the phone. But with AI now better able to mimic a real human voice, being able to tell the difference between a customer and an AI working on the customer’s behalf will get tougher.

Despite the challenges, this feature is one that could appeal to consumers who have grown frustrated with the state of customer service over the years. For too long, customers have been forced to navigate complex phone trees, and then sit on hold waiting for a human representative to pick up. If they’re lucky, that person can actually help them, and not transfer them to another department. Now, those consumers could potentially use their AI, with its infinite patience, for this tedious task instead.

While calls remain a paid-only feature for the time being, the recently added option to make a Muse email address will soon roll out to free users, Wang said this week.

#191. Judge Allows Trump’s Defamation Lawsuit Against the BBC to Proceed

President Trump is seeking damages from the British broadcaster over a documentary it conceded was misleadingly edited.

A federal judge in Florida on Tuesday allowed President Trump’s defamation lawsuit against the BBC to proceed, rejecting the British broadcaster’s bid to dismiss it.

Mr. Trump sued the BBC over its documentary “Trump: A Second Chance?” which aired a week before the 2024 presidential election. The program spliced together two portions of remarks that Mr. Trump delivered roughly 55 minutes apart on Jan. 6, 2021, giving the impression that he was encouraging his supporters to storm the U.S. Capitol.

Mr. Trump’s legal team has accused the BBC of “intentionally, maliciously and deceptively doctoring his speech” in an effort to interfere with the election, and is seeking $10 billion in damages.

The BBC has apologized, acknowledging that its editing gave “the mistaken impression that Trump made a direct call for violent action,” a controversy that prompted the resignations of its director general, Tim Davie, and news chief, Deborah Turness. But it says it was not evidence of a fundamental bias in the BBC’s news reporting and did not constitute defamation.

On Truth Social, Mr. Trump described Tuesday’s ruling as a “A Huge Victory against Fake News BBC.”

A spokeswoman for the BBC said in an emailed statement: “We are surprised by this decision but respect the court’s authority and note the case will now proceed to the next stage. We remain confident in our position and will continue to respond through the proper legal process.”

Lawyers for the BBC had sought to have the lawsuit dismissed, in part because the program was never aired in the United States.

#190. Dogecoin jumps after Elon Musk replaces Twitter bird with Shiba Inu

Twitter’s traditional bird icon was booted and replaced with an image of a Shiba Inu, an apparent nod to dogecoin, the joke cryptocurrency that CEO Elon Musk is being sued over.

Musk addressed the change Monday afternoon, tweeting, “as promised” above an image of a year-old conversation in which another user suggested that Musk “just buy Twitter” and “change the bird logo to a doge.”

Elon Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out one account

The doge logo appeared on the site two days after Musk asked a judge to throw out a $258 billion racketeering lawsuit accusing him of running a pyramid scheme to support the dogecoin, according to Reuters.

Lawyers for Musk and Tesla called the lawsuit by dogecoin investors a “fanciful work of fiction” over Musk’s “innocuous and often silly tweets.”

It wasn’t clear whether the logo change was permanent. Musk has been known to use Twitter to troll both his fans and his critics.

The price of dogecoin, which is typically volatile, was up more than 20% over the past 24 hours, to about 9 cents. It was trading just under 8 cents Monday morning.

Dogecoin was created December 6, 2013, by a pair of software engineers — as a joke. The name is a nod to the “doge” meme that became popular a decade ago. Its Shiba Inu mascot mimicks that meme: a dog surrounded by a bunch of Comic Sans text in broken English.

#187. Democrats launch Nvidia investigation, write scathing letter on Huang’s ‘scheme’ with Trump

Democrats on Thursday opened an inquiry into how the Trump administration handled Nvidia’s chip sales to China. Earlier on Thursday, the company ousted its CEO Jensen Huang. He also agreed to testify to a different set of Republican-led Congressional committees.

Huang was removed from the company he founded after he confessed to an extramarital affair with a woman accused of being a Chinese spy. (He has since been accused of having more affairs, with Nvidia employees.)

The alleged Chinese spy, Alice Huang, was an executive of Nvidia customer Megaspeed, a Singapore-based company that U.S. officials suspect of helping route advanced Nvidia chips into China. The Democratic inquiry comes from Rep. Richard Neal (D-Mass.), the top Democrat on the House Ways and Means Committee, and Sen. Ron Wyden (D-Ore.), the top Democrat on the Senate Finance Committee. As the minority party, they can ask questions but can’t compel answers.

Their humdinger of a letter to Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick asks the department to preserve records about an arrangement they find questionable and, they allege, illegal.

“The scheme worked like this,” as the letter describes it. President Trump agreed to sales of Nvidia’s H200 chips to China as long as the chips, manufactured by Taiwan, were first shipped to the United States. On arriving, Trump added a tariff “effectively taking a cut of Nvidia’s sales to China through what appears to be an unconstitutional export tax system,” the letter said. Then the Commerce Department, under Lutnick, issued an export license.

By the way, the deal between Trump and Nvidia to share revenue was public. In August 2025, Nvidia and AMD both agreed to share 15% of their revenue from chip sales to China with the U.S. government as part of a deal to secure export licenses, the AP reported at the time.

But the letter questions why Lutnick continued to allow Megaspeed to buy chips even after he was reportedly informed by U.S. intelligence of their suspicions of Alice Huang and of Jensen Huang’s relationship with her. It questions why the department reportedly green-lit other Asian companies to buy Nvidia chips “with no physical presence at their listed addresses.” So the inquiry is aimed at the administration, not just at Nvidia and Huang.

The letter also questions how Nvidia’s donations to Trump’s projects factored in. It notes that the export deal was approved after Trump learned of “Mr. Huang’s compromised position” in sleeping with an alleged spy, and after Nvidia donated $26 million toward Trump’s projects, including the ballroom. It appears to be asking if Trump used his knowledge of the affair to secure donations from Nvidia.

Interestingly, in a “60 Minutes” interview with President Trump that aired on Sunday, Trump mentioned how he and his team had threatened an unnamed AI exec with jail, and after that, the exec “totally changed.”

This Congressional investigation into Nvidia’s China sales and its dealings with Trump is the second one, so far, since news of the alleged spy affair broke. The Republican-led group involves leaders from the House Select Committee on the Chinese Communist Party, the House Committee on Foreign Affairs, and the House Intelligence Committee. That group has demanded that Huang come to them and testify in a closed-door session. They had threatened to subpoena him if he didn’t voluntarily agree. On Thursday, after his resignation, he agreed.

#185. Protesters Throw Projectiles at Biden During Dublin Trip

Video showed demonstrators from a pro-Palestinian protest throwing projectiles at former President Joseph R. Biden Jr. after he attended an event at Trinity College Dublin on Tuesday.

Protesters threw projectiles at former President Joseph R. Biden Jr. after he attended an event at Trinity College Dublin on Tuesday, as the police scuffled with demonstrators attending a pro-Palestinian rally, according to video of the incident.

An Garda Síochána, the Irish national police force, said three men had been arrested on Tuesday in relation to a public order incident. Two of the men were later released, and one was charged, the police said in a statement on Wednesday.

Video of the episode showed Mr. Biden being shielded from the projectiles by black umbrellas held by members of his entourage.

Mr. Biden had been attending a ceremony honoring his son, Hunter Biden, by a debating society that praised him for speaking publicly about politics and his struggle with addiction. Hunter Biden and Jill Biden, the former first lady, also attended the event, according to Ireland’s national broadcaster, RTÉ.

“A protest took place in Trinity College Dublin’s Front Square on Tuesday, 10 November during a ceremony in the Exam Hall,” said Catherine O’Mahony, a spokeswoman for the college. “The protest escalated while the Bidens were leaving” the event, she said.

Videos show groups of people gathered in the square, some waving Palestinian flags and chanting “blood on your hands.” As Mr. Biden was leaving the area, footage shows objects and white liquid, which appears to be milk, being thrown at him.

#183. Erdogan's political fate may be determined by Turkey's Kurds

Editor’s Note:  A version of this story first appeared in CNN’s Meanwhile in the Middle East newsletter, a three-times-a-week look inside the region’s biggest stories. Sign up here.

Turkey’s persecuted pro-Kurdish party has emerged as a kingmaker in the country’s upcoming election, playing a decisive role that may just tip the balance enough to unseat two-decade ruler Recep Tayyip Erdogan.

In a key setback to the Turkish president and leader of the Justice and Development Party (AK Party), the pro-Kurdish Peoples’ Democratic Party (HDP) last month announced that it would not put forward its own presidential candidate, a move analysts say allows its supporters to vote for Erdogan’s main rival.

“We are facing a turning point that will shape the future of Turkey and (its) society,” said the HDP in a statement on March 23. “To fulfill our historical responsibility against the one-man rule, we will not field a presidential candidate in (the) May 14 elections.”

It is a twist of irony for the Turkish strongman, who spent the better half of the past decade cracking down on the party after it began chipping away at his voter base. Its former leader Selahattin Demirtas has been in prison for nearly seven years and the party faces possible closure by a court for suspected collusion with the militant Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK) and affiliated groups. But its influence may nonetheless determine the course of Turkey’s politics.

The HDP’s decision not to field a candidate came just three days after head of the Republican People’s Party (CHP) Kemal Kilicdaroglu, Erdogan’s main rival, visited the party’s co-chairs. He told reporters that the solution to Turkey’s problems, “including the Kurdish problem” lies in parliament,” according to Turkish media.

Kilicdaroglu, who represents the six-party Nation Alliance opposition bloc, is the strongest contender to run against Erdogan in years. And while the HDP hasn’t yet announced whether it will put its weight behind him, analysts say it is the kingmaker in the elections.

Erdogan's 'polar opposite' wants to replace him as president of Turkey

“It was a carefully crafted political discourse,” Hisyar Ozsoy, deputy co-chair of the HDP and a member of parliament from the predominantly Kurdish province of Diyarbakir, told CNN. “We are not going to have our own candidate, and we will leave it to the international community to interpret it the way they wish.”

Experts say the crackdown on the HDP is rooted in the threat it poses to Erdogan politically, as well as its position as one of the main parties representing Turkey’s Kurds, an ethnic minority from which a separatist militant movement has emerged.

The party and the Kurdish people have had a complicated relationship with Erdogan. The leader courted the Kurds in earlier years by granting them more rights and reversing restrictions on the use of their language. Relations with the HDP were also cordial once, as Erdogan worked with the party on a brief peace process with the PKK.

But ties between Erdogan and the HDP later turned sour, and the HDP fell under a sweeping crackdown aimed at the PKK and their affiliates.

Kurds are the biggest minority in Turkey, making up between 15% and 20% of the population, according to Minority Rights Group International.

It is unclear if the HDP will endorse Kilicdaroglu, but analysts say that the deliberate distance may be beneficial for the opposition candidate.

The accusations against the HDP place it in a precarious position during the elections. It currently faces a case in Turkey’s Constitutional Court over suspected ties to the PKK, which is designated as a terrorist group by Turkey, the United States and the European Union. Knowing it may be banned at any moment, its candidates are running under the Green Left Party in parliament.

If the opposition is seen as allying with the HDP, Erdogan’s AK Party may use its influence in the media to discredit it as being pro-PKK, said Murat Somer, a political science professor at Koc University in Istanbul and author of Return to Point Zero, a book on the Turkish-Kurdish question in Turkey.

The HDP’s threat to Erdogan’s hold on power became apparent after the June 2015 election, the first general election it participated in. It won 13% of the seats, denying the ruling AK Party its majority for the first time since 2002. Erdogan, however, called a snap election five months later, which led to a drop in the HDP’s support to 10.7%, as well as the restoration of the AK Party’s overall majority.

“They are a kingmaker in these elections because the HDP gets about half of the votes of the Kurdish population in Turkey,” said Somer, adding that the other, more conservative Kurdish voters have traditionally voted for Erdogan’s AK Party. And last month, the Free Cause Party (HUDA-PAR), a tiny Kurdish-Islamist party announced support for Erdogan in the elections. The party has never won seats in parliament.

Erdogan's political fate may rest on his response to the earthquake

The HDP knows that its position is key to the outcome of next month’s vote, but that it’s also in a delicate situation.

“We want to play the game wisely, and we need to be very careful,” said Ozsoy, adding that the party wants to avoid a “contaminated political climate” where the elections are polarized “between a very ugly ultra-nationalist discourse against Kilicdaroglu and others.”

The party was founded in 2012 with a number of aims, said Ozsoy, one of which was “peaceful and democratic resolution of the Kurdish conflict.”

Somer said that the party was seen to be “an initiative” of the PKK, which later led to a heavy government crackdown on it in the name of counterterrorism.

Its former leader Demirtas remains an influential figure.

The Turkish government has been trying to link the HDP to the PKK but has so far failed to prove “a real connection,” said Asli Aydintasbas, a visiting fellow at the Brookings Institution in Washington, DC.

A post-Erdogan Turkey may give some breathing space to the Kurds and Kurdish-dominated parties in Turkey, Aydintasbas told CNN, noting that many Kurdish voters have recently left Erdogan’s camp. “For HDP, this is more than just an ideological choice,” she said. “It’s a matter of survival.”

Ozsoy says his party understands what’s at stake, not only for Turkey’s Kurds but for all its minorities.

“We are aware of our responsibility here. We are aware of our role. We know we are in a kingmaker position,” the HDP lawmaker said.

Oil prices spiked Monday after OPEC+ producers unexpectedly announced that they would cut output. #180. Calls grow for release of beloved California nurse detained by ICE: ‘We want her home’

Ann Lawani was taken from her home, sparking a huge outcry among students and faculty at her university

Nearly two months after ICE agents whisked a beloved California nurse and professor from her home in Lodi, her university has ramped up calls for her release.

“She needs to come home,” said Eileen Fry-Bowers, dean for the University of San Francisco’s nursing school,in reference to Ann Lawani, who was arrested by immigration agents in September and has since been shuffled between detention centers in California, Texas, and Louisiana, in a series of opaque transfers.

Lawani, 39, came to the US in 2007. She established herself as a nursing professional, obtaining a master’s and doctoral degree in the field, before stepping into academia.

She steers two programs at the University of San Francisco, one a master’s entry program geared toward students with non-nursing backgrounds and another a bachelor’s program for students seeking to work in public health safety-net institutions.

“She basically has her hands on almost 300 students that she engages with on a regular basis,” Fry-Bowers said.

Lawani’s detention has sparked outcry from local lawmakers, who have advocated on her behalf to ICE authorities, as well as her students and colleagues, who say her absence is deeply felt on campus.

One of those students, Casey Murray, a nursing science graduate student, described Lawani as a warm educator who could recall the first and last names of pupils she had not seen since orientation.

“We have 37 days left until we graduate,” Murray said. “She has supported us to the fullest extent up until the point that she was kidnapped. We want her at our graduation, and we want her home with her family.”

Federal immigration officials say Lawani entered the US on a visitor’s visa in 2007, which she overstayed. She was issued a final order of removal in August 2013, according to a Department of Homeland Security (DHS) spokesperson.

Natalia Santanna, Lawani’s lawyer, confirmed the removal order, but noted that DHS’s account was “incomplete”.

“Dr Lawani applied for asylum within one year of being in the United States. And, in the same 2013 order, the immigration judge found that Ann would more likely than not face persecution in Nigeria and granted withholding of removal,” Santanna said. “That is not a temporary stay. It is a continuing bar to removal to Nigeria. Dr Lawani has lived in the United States pursuant to that grant of withholding of removal since 2013.”

Santanna said they have filed a habeas petition to challenge the legality of her detainment, which remains pending in California’s eastern district court.

Meanwhile, Lawani’s students and colleagues are continuing to mobilize around her.

Lawani sent Fry-Bowers a text about being in ICE’s custody, shortly after her arrest. Fry-Bowers, who was on vacation, heard from another dean that Lawani had been absent from a meeting, which she described as out of character.

After the school heard from Lawani’s family, they jumped into action, aiding with legal representation and registering their alarm with the area’s congressional delegation.

In late October, Josh Harder, a congressman, who represents Lawani’s district, expressed his concerns about her case to DHS secretary Markwayne Mullin, among them the lack of notice before her arrest and her various whereabouts not being reflected in an online detainee database.

“Given her longstanding compliance, lack of criminal history, and substantial community ties, there is no apparent public safety justification for keeping her detained thousands of miles from home while her case proceeds,” Harder wrote then in a letter.

Fry-Bowers praised Lawani, citing her work with patients in hospice and palliative care.

“She cares for the most vulnerable, helps them maintain their dignity and comfort as they prepare for death,” she said. “She is a consummate professional. The highest praise I could ever give a nursing colleague is that I would want them taking care of my family member or myself, and she would be at the top of the list.”

#179. Chris Van Hollen to challenge Chuck Schumer for Senate majority leader

Maryland Democrat challenges party leader after midterms sweep, saying: ‘The American people called for change’

Chris Van Hollen, the Democratic senator of Maryland, announced on Thursday that he is challenging Chuck Schumer for the role of Senate majority leader when Democrats regain the chamber’s majority in January.

“In this election, the American people called for change. They called for a change from Donald Trump’s disastrous policies – but even bigger than that, a change from the status quo that isn’t working for working people,” Van Hollen said in a statement posted on social media.

“It’s now on us to meet this moment, and to do that we need new leadership. This is not about any one person – it’s about answering the call of the American people. That is why I’m running for Senate Majority Leader.”

Schumer, the leader of the Democratic minority in the current Senate, responded less than a minute later in a social media post of his own. “I welcome any discussion about the future of our caucus and the important work ahead,” Schumer wrote. “I’m proud of what we’ve accomplished together – 55 seats and counting is an amazing result – and I have the votes to continue serving as Democratic Leader. I look forward to leading our new Democratic majority and delivering for the American people.”

The battle pits a progressive Democrat, Van Hollen, against a centrist, Schumer.

Van Hollen’s challenge comes after he said he was considering a bid and after Schumer said he had the necessary support to win. The closed-door, secret-ballot election will be held in early December.

The race will be the first contested election for Democratic leader in decades. The question of whether to re-elect Schumer has already divided the caucus and many senators will not say publicly how they will vote. They have privately deliberated about what message to take from the election, and Schumer recruited many of the party’s winning candidates and raised money for them. But some of the winning candidates, most notably Michigan’s senator-elect, Abdul El-Sayed, did not initially have Schumer’s support.

Schumer has been talking to Democratic senators since the election about forming an agenda for 2027.

“I’m going to win it,” Schumer told the Associated Press in an interview last week.

#178. A.I. and Market Whiplash

A Times reporter who covers financial markets helps us make sense of tech turbulence.

Last week’s midterm elections were perhaps the first major elections fought at least in part over issues related to artificial intelligence. This year saw record donations from tech executives to tech-friendly candidates. And in some cases, A.I. was actually on the ballot: In local ballot initiatives across the country, people voted to limit or prevent the development of data centers. Many newly elected Democrats have promised A.I. regulation.

If A.I. has surged to the forefront of U.S. politics, it’s in part because A.I. is at the forefront of the U.S. economy. And stock markets around the world rise and fall with A.I. shares, particularly the multitrillion-dollar behemoths based in Silicon Valley. I spoke to my colleague Joe Rennison, who covers financial markets, about the dramatic swings in tech stocks following the midterms and what they mean — for Americans, and for the rest of us.

So, Joe. We’ve seen some serious volatility in the stock market after the midterms. First markets rallied, then they had their worst day in nearly two years. This week, as Nvidia stock plunged because of the Jensen Huang affair, the rest of the market bounced back.

What’s going on, and how much of all this is down to politics?

Certainly some of it is down to politics. The initial rally after the midterms came in part from investors who had parked their cash on the sidelines, in case of some unexpected event — say, a contested election. They bought back in when it became evident we were going to get a clear outcome.

The plunge the next day, though, came after some weaker than expected financial reports from a couple of A.I. companies, and after investors began processing what Democratic control of Congress and some Democratic wins at the state level might actually mean for the A.I. industry.

#177. Israeli military veterans, a backbone of protest movement, vow to keep demonstrating

In a sea of Israeli flags, Yiftach Golov holds one that looks a little different.

Among the hundreds of thousands of protesters who took to the streets for the 13th week in a row on Saturday, Golov hoists a brown flag that represents a group called “Brother and Sisters in Arms.”

They are veterans – many, like Golov, from elite forces – who now feel they are fighting on a new battlefield: To save Israeli democracy.

“We believe this is our responsibility to go once again called to the flag of the nation to stop this madness to defend Israel,” Golov said, as he weaved his way through the protesters on Tel Aviv’s Kaplan street, between the high-rises that house many of Israel’s high tech companies.

During the second intifada, in the early 2000s, Golov served in a special forces reconnaissance unit. He was never before particularly political, focusing more on getting his PhD in biophysics from Tel Aviv University.

But when the protest movement against the Israeli government’s judicial overhaul plan began in January, Golov attended one a demonstration and soon became one of thousands of veterans, and now military reservists, who have taken up the cause as their new mission.

Some, including elite Air Force reservists, have taken it a step further, threatening not to heed the call to train or even serve in protest of the government’s plans planned judicial changes, which would give the governing parties more control over Israel’s judiciary.

Others have taken to becoming some of the most active organizers and demonstrators. Last week, a group from Brothers and Sisters in Arms protested by carrying a figure wrapped in the Israeli flag on a stretcher, the way they would carry a wounded comrade off the field.

While Golov says he has not taken the drastic step to refuse service, he understands the motivation.

“We’re fighting for justice and liberty, just like the American story, that’s the values that that are being represented symbolized back when we look at our flag, that’s something that was lacking lacking for the last few decades. So basically, we reclaim the flag,” he said.

Palestinian taxi driver recounts surviving attack by right-wing protesters

Fellow members of the group, all wearing brown shirts with the organization’s logo, come up and say hello. They’re sprinkled all throughout the protests. One is even leading the “Pink Front,” a group of coordinated drummers who look like they are dressed for a rave, and often lead the chants at the protests.

They’re using skills they learned in the military – how to organize, how to mobilize – now for the protests. But more importantly, they say they have the same type of motivation.

“The very deep feeling that you are part of something bigger than yourself, that (you’re) allowed to sacrifice anything that is needed, whether it’s your career, health, seriously mental health,” Golov said. “We all have a mission, you’re willing to do it at any cost. You’re very determined, you know that you are on the right side, you’re carrying the torch of light. That keeps us being highly motivated despite the fact that we’re not sleeping for days.”

Israel’s protest movement is made up of many disparate groups, but the pressure from Israel’s much vaunted veterans has been seen as a key to moving the needle.

Last Monday, after weeks of sustained protests and the largest general strike in Israeli history, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu announced a pause to the legislation, to allow time for negotiations with the opposition.

But despite the announcements, protesters are still out in the streets in large numbers. CNN affiliate Channel 12 in Israel estimated the size of Saturday’s demonstration in Tel Aviv at about 150,000 people. Organizers claimed it was 230,000.

Last week’s mass protests and widespread strike action came after Netanyahu said he had decided to fire Defense Minister Yoav Gallant for advocating a delay in passing the legislation – a move that Netanyahu has since delayed, sources told CNN, due to “the present security situation.”

In his televised speech calling for a delay, Gallant had said the pause in the legislation was needed “for the security of Israel,” citing the refusal of some Israel Defense Forces reservists to train in protest of the government plans. He said pressing ahead with the proposals could threaten Israel’s security.

Under pressure at home and from allies abroad, Netanyahu said he would delay votes on the remaining legislation until after the Knesset’s Passover recess in April “to give time for a real chance for a real debate.”

“Out of the responsibility to the nation, I decided to delay … the vote, in order to give time for discussion,” he added.

But Netanyahu indicated that the delay was only temporary. He insisted that the overhaul was necessary, and reiterated criticism of refusal to train or serve in the military in protest at the planned changes. “Refusing is the end of our country,” he said.

Many protesters don’t believe that the pause is real, or say it’s simply a stalling tactic to give Netanyahu some breathing room and get the protesters to go home before he plows on with the reforms.

“We will start doing deactivation only when we will know 100% that Israel state will stay a functional democratic country. Whatever needs to be done for that,” Golov said.

#173. Who is Colette Kress, interim Nvidia CEO after Jensen Huang exit?

Chief financial officer, reportedly disparaged by Huang during summer meeting, to take over on short-term basis

The hunt is on for a new Nvidia CEO. It comes after a whirlwind week, which saw the company head and co-founder, Jensen Huang, resign amid multiple allegations of extramarital affairs, including one that involved an alleged Chinese spy and two with company employees.

Nvidia immediately named Colette Kress, the company’s chief financial officer, as the interim CEO. It’s possible she could stay on permanently, but the company could also name another candidate for the full-time role. Names that have been floated include Ian Buck, who manages Nvidia’s AI hardware and software products, and Rene Haas, a former Nvidia employee and the current CEO of Arm Holdings, a UK-based chipmaker.

Whoever takes over from Huang will have tremendous sway in the burgeoning world of artificial intelligence. The entire AI ecosphere relies on Nvidia, which engineers advanced chips used to train AI models and power companies such as OpenAI, Anthropic and Google. Nvidia is also a central driver of the US economy and one of the world’s most valuable companies, with a market cap of around $4tn.

Huang, who co-founded the company in 1993, is known as an ambitious and visionary CEO who had a hand in most all aspects of the business. He led in an unusual management style, having about 60 direct reports, giving him more control over the company’s daily functions. His son and daughter also work at Nvidia, with his daughter seen as his possible proxy, but neither appear to have sufficient experience to take the helm.

Kress, 59, joined Nvidia in 2013 as the CFO after working in finance roles at Texas Instruments, Microsoft and Cisco. She’s a regular contributor to the company’s quarterly earnings calls, and has overseen Nvidia go from a niche firm geared toward video game chips to a trillion-dollar AI juggernaut. People who know her describe Kress as a calming contrast to Huang’s often confrontational style, Reuters reported.

“Colette has helped lead Nvidia through a period of extraordinary growth and has a deep understanding of our business, customers, operations and financial position,” Harvey C Jones, the newly named chair of Nvidia’s board of directors, said in a statement on Thursday. “The board has full confidence in Colette and the leadership team.”

But that sentiment may not be shared by Huang. During a meeting this past summer, the CEO said Kress was “a deer in the headlights” and interrupted her presentation, a person familiar with the comments told the Wall Street Journal. Huang insinuated during that August meeting that Kress was not someone who could help him make decisions.

However, Kress praised Huang in a memo to employees on Thursday, saying she would continue “building on the extraordinary foundation Jensen created over the last 33 years”.

Many investors, analysts and industry sources who spoke to Reuters questioned whether a finance executive could preserve the technological risk-taking that turned Nvidia into an AI chips powerhouse.

“Putting [Kress] in the interim role makes sense to keep the markets calm, but I don’t think she is the long-term visionary that Nvidia needs to continue its current trajectory,” said Anshel Sag, principal analyst at Moor Insights and Strategy.

Kress, who said in her memo that Nvidia’s “strength has always come from its people” , will serve as both the CEO and interim CFO until the board names a permanent successor.

The board said it was starting a search for Huang’s permanent replacement and is considering internal and external candidates. Along with Kress, Buck is seen as a possible successor. He has worked at Nvidia since 2004 and in charge of deploying generative AI in partnership with the world’s top AI companies, including Microsoft, Google, Amazon, Meta, OpenAI and more.

Haas, the CEO of Arm Holdings, is also well-known at Nvidia, having worked there for seven years as a vice president and general manager before joining Arm in 2013. Lisa Su, the CEO of chipmaker AMD, is also being named as a possible replacement for Huang. Her company is Nvidia’s most direct and fierce competitor; she’s also a distant cousin to Huang.

Sunny Cheung, an analyst at the Washington-based Jamestown Foundation, said Kress could face congressional questions about the company’s oversight of chip sales involving Chinese military-linked institutions and south-east Asian intermediaries.

In a 2023 interview with an Israeli publication, Kress credited her two young sons with urging her to join Nvidia in 2013 because they knew its chips from their video games.

She became a billionaire in 2025 based on the value of her Nvidia equity, according to media reports, and Forbes said her net worth of $1.2 billion in mid-2026 put her in 32nd place on its list of America’s richest self-made women.

Kress, who holds an undergraduate degree in finance and an MBA, was also named among the 50 most powerful women in business on rankings compiled by Fortune and Forbes magazines in recent years.

Nvidia’s CEO turmoil comes as the company is set to report its third-quarter earnings next Tuesday.

#172. Chris Van Hollen says he will run for Senate majority leader, challenging fellow Democrat Chuck Schumer – as it happened

Chris Van Hollen to challenge Chuck Schumer for Senate majority leader

Senator Chris Van Hollen, a Maryland Democrat, announced on Thursday that he is challenging Chuck Schumer for the role of Senate majority leader when Democrats regain the majority in January.

“In this election, the American people called for change. They called for a change from Donald Trump’s disastrous policies – but even bigger than that, a change from the status quo that isn’t working for working people,” Van Hollen said in a statement posted on social media. “It’s now on us to meet this moment, and to do that we need new leadership. This is not about any one person – it’s about answering the call of the American people. That is why I’m running for Senate Majority Leader.”

Schumer, the leader of the Democratic minority in the current Senate, responded less than a minute later in a social media post of his own. “I welcome any discussion about the future of our caucus and the important work ahead,” Schumer wrote. “I’m proud of what we’ve accomplished together—55 seats and counting is an amazing result—and I have the votes to continue serving as Democratic Leader. I look forward to leading our new Democratic majority and delivering for the American people.”

The battle pits a progressive Democrat, Van Hollen, against a centrist, Schumer, in the wake of Senate wins for progressive Democrats in Michigan and Maine who were not initially supported by Schumer.

The contest will also present Democratic senators with a choice between two modes of confronting Donald Trump. Schumer is a cautious politician who has been widely criticized by Democratic voters for failing to stand up to Trump. Van Hollen, by contrast, took on the president’s mass deportation effort head on when he visited El Salvador in early 2025 and demanded to check on the condition of Kilmar Ábrego, a constituent who was wrongly deported to the country.

Van Hollen, by contrast, took on the president’s mass deportation effort head on when he visited El Salvador in early 2025 and demanded to check on the condition of Kilmar Ábrego, a constituent who was wrongly deported to the country.

Congressman Jamie Raskin, a Maryland Democrat and the incoming chair of the House judiciary committee, told reporters on Thursday that one of his first priorities will be investigating the Trump administration firings of career justice department employees.

“That’s something that is central,” Raskin said of the firings that have taken place over the last two years.

The Maryland congressman said he expects, with majorities in both chambers of Congress, Democrats will have more authority to hold the administration accountable through the “power of the purse.”

Raskin is in Denver with other Democrats to discuss the next steps after winning majorities in the House and Senate.

In a friendly, softball interview with the sports pundit Stephen A Smith on Thursday at the White House, Donald Trump defended the Iran war with a big lie, and deflected blame for his party’s midterm losses with misinformation.

On Iran, Trump falsely claimed, as he has repeatedly, that the war he started in February was essential to prevent Iran from obtaining a nuclear weapon.

In fact, US intelligence agencies reported to Congress in 2025 that it was their assessment that “Iran is not building a nuclear weapon” and that the country’s supreme leader at the time, Ayatollah Ali Khamenei “has not reauthorized the nuclear weapons program he suspended in 2003”.

Three months after that assessment was written, Trump ordered the US bombing of suspected uranium enrichment sites in Iran, which could be used for peaceful energy purposes or weapons making, and declared in June 2025 that “Iran’s key nuclear enrichment facilities have been completely and totally obliterated.”

Speaking to Smith on Thursday, however, Trump claimed that the war he started with Iran just eight months later, in February, was necessary because of “the nuclear weapon that they were two weeks away from having”.

There is no public evidence that Iran had restarted the nuclear weapons program it suspended 23 years ago, but the rhetorical device of claiming that the regime was close to having a weapon has been deployed, for three decades, by Israel’s prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu.

In the same interview, the president also sought to deflect blame from himself for the losses by Republican candidates for the US Senate in at least nine states he visited for campaign rallies in the closing weeks of the midterm campaign: North Carolina, South Carolina, Georgia, Mississippi, Texas, Kansas, Iowa, Michigan and Ohio. (He also visited Alaska to campaign for the Republican incumbent, who seems to be on track to lose as well.)

Asked by Smith what happened in the elections, Trump pointed to the candidates and reminded Smith that he won all of those states as a presidential candidate two years ago (before his popularity plummeted as a result of his decision to raise the cost of goods and fuel for Americans by imposing tariffs on imports and then attacking Iran).

He then suggested that he was still popular in all of those formerly Republican states that responded to his rallies by voting to reject the candidates he campaigned for. “I think I still have it,” Trump told Smith. “You saw with the rallies, I mean, the rallies were packed. We’d have 15, 20, 25,000 people at every rally,” he added, repeating a false claim he made at most of those rallies, which were, in fact, held in smaller venues, not all of which were filled to capacity.

In San Antonio, Texas, for instance, a spokesperson for the the venue Trump spoke at, Freeman Coliseum, told reporters the White House requested a three-quarter-house setup, so the hall was set up with 6,844 seats. In Greenville, North Carolina, he spoke to 5,000 people at the Pitt-Greenville Airport a fire marshal told the local News & Observer. In Port Huron, Michigan, he packed a small hockey arena with a capacity of 3,000 people. In Chillicothe, Ohio, Trump claimed that there were 42,000 people in attendance at a university sports center that holds 2,400 spectators.

Taking time out of his busy schedule of posting on social media, Donald Trump sat down for a lengthy podcast interview on Thursday with the sports pundit Stephen A Smith to express sympathy for Jensen Huang, who resigned as chief executive of Nvidia after reports of extramarital affairs with an alleged Chinese spy and two company employees.

Asked by Smith, a bombastic commentator who has floated the idea of entering politics himself, if Huang should have stepped down, Trump sighed deeply before saying: “Well, the big thing is, was the woman a spy? And I don’t know if that’s been determined yet, but if the woman was a spy, yes. If the woman was not a spy, perhaps it would be different.”

Trump also defended Huang, who sat at his table at a White House state dinner for China’s president, Xi Jinping, in September as “a tremendous talent”.

“I can tell you this, somehow he’ll end up in his feet,” Trump added. “I mean, I know him; I know him pretty well. I always thought of him as a fine man.”

“I spoke to him yesterday,” he president continued. “He’s he’s going through hell.”

“He called me as the president – I’m president – and he called me and he said, ‘What I’ve done to my wife and family is, is- that’s what he talked about,” Trump told Smith.

A return to normal politics: Darline Graham meets with South Carolina senator-elect Annie Andrews

During the final stretch of South Carolina’s Senate race, Darline Graham laced into her Democratic opponent Annie Andrews, calling her “bat shit crazy,” at a rally with Donald Trump, in what came off as a feeble attempt to match the president’s bravado.

After Andrews’s stunning win, in a seat left vacant by Graham’s late brother, Lindsey Graham, the two appear to be civil.

Graham smiled as she stood next to Andrews, in a Thursday photograph on X.

“I am committed to ensuring a smooth transition, and I wish her and her team the best,” Graham captioned the post.

Democrat Cindy Holscher has been elected Kansas’ next governor, narrowly defeating Republican Ty Masterson to keep the office in her party’s hands and extend a massive wave of Democratic victories across the country.

Democrats had already secured a majority of governorships in US states for the first time in two decades, even prior to the narrow win in Kansas, where Holscher had already declared victory but the Associated Press did not call the race until Thursday.

Holscher, a state senator, promised to halt new data centers and overcame a flood of money that Republicans poured behind Masterson, the president of the Republican-controlled state senate. She will succeed outgoing governor Laura Kelly, a Democrat who was barred from running for a third term by term limits.

Kelly, a centrist Democrat, had opposed the more progressive Holscher in the Democratic primary, and endorsed her rival, state senators Ethan Corson, but campaigned for the party’s nominee in the general election.

In a campaign ad supporting Holscher last month, Kelly described Masteron as an extremist and assured voters the Democrat would “govern from the middle”.

Masterson, who was endorsed by Donald Trump, remains the Kansas state senate’s Republican leader.

Retaining the governor’s mansion was the third in a series of big wins for Kansas Democrats, after Adam Hamilton was elected to the US Senate, defeating the Trump-backed incumbent Roger Marshall, and Chris Mann defeated the state’s incumbent Republican attorney general Kris Kobach.

Kobach has helped sow doubt about US elections and served as vice-chair of Trump’s short-lived commission on election fraud in 2017, when he tried, and failed, to find evidence to support Trump’s baseless claim that he had not lost the popular vote in the 2016 election.

Mann, a former police officer, had narrowly lost to Kobach in 2022.

Rob Sand, the governor-elect of Iowa, said Democrats should be cautious about spending too much time pursuing Trump administration investigations, claiming voters want to see progress, not rehashing of old wounds.

“Every time I read, right now, about more investigations of things that have already been reported on in the media, I’m thinking, why do we need to investigate that? It’s already been reported on. Why don’t you do something actually productive or better people’s lives,” Sand told reporters on Thursday in Denver at the What’s Next conference on the future of the Democratic party.

Sand recalled that while serving in the Iowa State Auditor’s office, the team typically did not pursue investigations that had been fully reported on because the public was already aware of potential corruption or misuse of taxpayer funds.

“I think that’s a pretty simple guideline for people,” Sand said. “Put your time into doing something that’s actually going to improve people’s lives and not just remind them of a bad thing that the other side did.”

Sand, who largely campaigned on a platform of being independent and called for the end of the two-party system, said he’s chosen to come to the What’s Next conference because he liked the organization behind it, Majority Democrats.

“I’m here for a day,” Sand said. “I think it’s important to talk about what we did in Iowa and anytime I can spread the message about making politics, making government, not redder or bluer, but redder and truer, I want to take that opportunity.”

While votes are still being counted in one tightly contested Senate election, the race in Alaska where Democrat Mary Peltola now holds a 3,975 vote lead over the nearest Dan Sullivan – the one who is the Republican incumbent – the outgoing Republican majority in Washington is using its final weeks in charge to stack the federal courts with as many judges nominated by Donald Trump as possible.

The Senate has already voted on Thursday to confirm one conservative, Gregory Cook, to be a US district judge in Alabama, and to move to final votes on Trump’s nominations of two judges he previously installed on the federal bench – Daniel Domenico and Anna St John – to serve on federal appeals courts.

The vote on St John’s nomination began with a flub by the senator presiding over the Senate, Darline Graham, the South Carolina Republican who is still serving out the remainder of her late brother Lindsey’s term, which ends in January, despite losing the seat to Democrat Annie Andrews last week.

Reading aloud from a piece of paper in front of her, Graham called the vote to end debate on St John’s nomination by mistaking the abbreviation for saint in her name, with the one for street. “The question is,” Graham said, “is it the sense of the Senate that debate on the nomination of Anna Street John of Louisiana to be United States judge for the fifth circuit shall be brought to a close?”

The Alliance for Justice (AFJ), a progressive judicial advocacy group, strongly opposed all three nominees.

Cook, who worked as a volunteer attorney in the 2000 Bush v Gore legal battle, is described by the AFJ as “a lifelong conservative who has consistently aligned himself with extremist ideologies in pursuit of more political power” whose “track record demonstrates a commitment to corporate interests and the spread of conservative ideology — not justice.”

Domenico, who is described by the AFJ as a potential supreme court nominee, was nominated to the US district court in Colorado by Trump in 2019. This year, Trump picked him to serve on the US court of appeals for the 10th circuit in Denver.

In January, when Trump nominated Anna St John to serve as a federal judge for the US district court for the Eastern District of Louisiana, the AFJ wrote that she “has spent her legal career silencing women, attacking LGBTQ+ rights, and cutting off corporate accountability for everyday people.” Before being confirmed to that role, and then nominated to a federal appeals court in New Orleans, St John led the Hamilton Legal Law Institute, which the AFJ calls “a far-right extremist organization that routinely files lawsuits to attack civil rights, undermine consumer protections, eliminate gender equality, and deny basic human rights”.

In her confirmation hearing for her elevation the the appeals court in August, St John refused to say that Trump lost the 2020 election and refrained from characterizing the January 6 2021 Capitol riot as an insurrection.

Democrats from across the country have gathered at the What’s Next conference in Denver to discuss what the party should do moving forward – now that they’ve secured majorities in the US House and Senate, and won control of the governor’s offices in a majority of states.

The conference, hosted by the group Majority Democrats, is bustling with excitement. In the outdoor space, there’s a six-piece jazz band playing upbeat variations of Americana songs, flanked by a colorful food truck.

The main stage event will be livestreamed, starting at 2:30 Mountain Time.

In the further annals of Donald Trump not changing a thing since last week’s midterm election defeat, he also mused earlier today on Truth Social: “Will the Seditious Six ever be prosecuted?”

“What they did was a great violation to our Country and its Constitution!” the president added.

It’s a reference to six Democratic lawmakers who last year published a video reminding members of the military and intelligence community that they can refuse illegal orders, amid concerns that Trump would attempt to use them against his political adversaries.

Here’s what senator Mark Kelly had to say about Trump’s latest threat:

double quotation markThe American people delivered a massive rejection of Donald Trump’s chaos and corruption last week. Now he’s again threatening to prosecute people who stand up to him because he’s afraid of being held accountable. Donald Trump has tried and failed to intimidate me for a year now. His threats won’t stop me from doing my job.

Immediately after Republicans suffered a drubbing in last week’s midterms, Donald Trump made some comments indicating he’d considering working with the incoming Democratic majorities in the Senate and House of Representatives.

Just about everything he’s done since – from appointing immigration hardliner Stephen Miller his chief of staff to threatening one of the top Democratic investigators – indicates he’s not looking to change much at all. Further evidence arrived this afternoon in the form of a Truth Social post, where the president says he’d like to work with Chuck Schumer on fighting corruption – but only in states led by Democrats.

“Billions of Dollars are being stolen on an hourly basis. Hopefully his words will ring true! It will be an honor to work with him on this very big Democrat problem that, when solved, will literally balance the Budget of the U.S.” the president writes.

Little he writes in that sentence appears to be true. There are 8,760 hours in a year, and the Government Accountability Office estimated in 2024 that between $233 bn to $521 bn are lost to fraud annually – so certainly not billions “on an hourly basis,” as Trump wrote. Democrats may have ridden voter concerns over the war with Iran to their largest congressional majority in years in last week’s midterm elections, but Donald Trump today announced that the US is no longer bothering to find peace with its Middle Eastern adversary. Here’s more, from the Guardian’s Andrew Roth:

Donald Trump has said that the US is no longer seeking a deal to end the war with Iran and ordered JD Vance, who had spearheaded recent efforts to restart negotiations with Iran, to focus on domestic politics instead.

In a post to his social media site on Thursday, Trump said he was redirecting the vice-president to focus his “complete and total attention on the Massive Corruption and Crime taking place in Democrat run Cities and States throughout the U.S”.

Vance had reportedly been talking to intermediaries in Iran and Qatar about restarting negotiations to end the war, which began in February when the US and Israel launched joint strikes on Iran. Iran responded by closing access to the strait of Hormuz, which led to a steep increase for the cost of fuel around the globe.

But on Thursday Trump appeared to shut down the negotiations, redirecting Vance to focus on domestic issues and downplaying the need for a final deal.

“We don’t need a deal with Iran,” Trump wrote. “Those days have long since passed. We’ve got their money, Hundreds of Billions of Dollars, and we’ve got their Strait!”

We reported earlier that Ukraine’s president has said that Starlink, the satellite internet service it relies to counter Russian drone attacks, has slowed down, hampering its defenses.

Democrats are already expected to investigate a number of the activities of Elon Musk, who controls Starlink, and whether the service is being deliberately throttled for Ukraine could be on their list. While many of these inquiries are sure to be strictly partisan affairs, Michael McCaul, an outgoing House Republican who supports defending Ukraine against Russia, just signaled his support for this particular line of questioning, writing the following on X:

double quotation markI am deeply concerned by reports that Ukraine’s access to Starshield has once again been restricted. These capabilities play a critical role in Ukraine’s defense against Putin’s relentless missile and drone attacks, which target civilian homes, parks, hospitals, and energy infrastructure.

With peace negotiations on the horizon, maintaining Ukraine’s momentum and leverage is more important than ever. I urge a full investigation into why these disruptions have now occurred twice and call for measures be put in place to ensure it does not happen again.

Donald Trump’s health secretary, Robert F Kennedy Jr, is facing potential legal trouble after a Democratic senator opened an inquiry into whether the lied to Congress, citing reporting by the Guardian. Here’s more, from the Guardian’s Michelle R Smith:

A top Democratic senator has launched an investigation into evidence that Robert F Kennedy Jr “unlawfully lied” in his 2025 confirmation hearings to become the US health secretary, citing a report by the Guardian.

Ron Wyden of Oregon, the incoming chair of the powerful Senate finance committee, is seeking extensive records from Children’s Health Defense (CHD), the non-profit anti-vaccine group Kennedy has used as a vehicle to fuel his political rise, including all documents related to a 2019 trip Kennedy took to Samoa.

“We are beginning an investigation into evidence that Secretary of Health and Human Services Robert F Kennedy, Jr unlawfully lied to Congress on multiple occasions,” Wyden said in a letter to the group that was sent on Wednesday and obtained by the Guardian. The letter was also signed by Angela Alsobrooks, a Democratic senator from Maryland.

In the letter, the two lawmakers cited reporting by the Guardian that indicated Kennedy lied about his reasons for going to Samoa in 2019, a trip that occurred just months before a measles outbreak killed 83 people in the Pacific island nation, mostly children under age five.

The trip, which Kennedy made as the chair and chief legal counsel of CHD, became one of the most criticized events in his career before he entered government. Critics, including Samoan officials, have said Kennedy’s presence emboldened anti-vaccine activists in Samoa, worsening a vaccination crisis. Kennedy has denied the allegations, saying he never gave any public statement about vaccines or discouraged Samoans from getting vaccinated.

The Trump administration faces an investigation of its scheme to take a 15% cut from any Nvidia chip sales going to China, as part of a deal to secure export licenses for the semiconductors.

Under the scheme, Nvidia chips made in Taiwan could be sold to Chinese companies if they were imported to the US first for a security review. This also made them subject to a 25% tariff under section 232 of the Trade Expansion Act of 1962, which was aimed at dealing with imports that threaten national security. The commerce secretary could then grant an export licence to sell the chips to China.

Two Democratic senators are now demanding Trump officials retain all records relating to the scheme as the fallout from now former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s affair with a suspected Chinese spy continues to grow.

Senate finance committee ranking member Ron Wyden and House ways and means committee ranking member Richard E. Neal said they were beginning to investigate following reports of the affair – which led to Huang’s resignation on Thursday. One focus of the investigation appear to be report that both commerce secretary Howard Lutnick and Donald Trump were briefed about the relationship but granted export approvals anyway.

double quotation markThe Trump administration’s ostensible attempt to mask an unconstitutional export tax as a national security tariff action – benefitting both Nvidia and the Chinese government – raises serious concerns within our committees’ jurisdiction over matters relating to taxation and tariffs.

Congress wrote Section 232 to give the president a tool to take action against imports he has determined to pose a national security threat; Trump’s use of the statute appears to have done the opposite, undermining US national security and putting Americans at risk.
